Forcing with Invariant Measures The third author’s research has been supported by a grant from IPM (No. 1402030417).
Nathanael Ackerman, Cameron Freer, Mohammad Golshani, Mostafa Mirabi, Rehana Patel

Abstract
This paper introduces a model-theoretic generalization of the notion of forcing with random reals, in which forcing gives rise to random generic structures . Specifically, we consider forcing with κ \kappa -Borel probability measures on the space of ℒ \Lang -structures with a (possibly uncountable) infinite set X X , focusing on those that are invariant under the action of the symmetric group Sym ⁡ ( X ) \Perm(X) . We demonstrate how any Sym ⁡ ( X ) \Perm(X) -invariant measure where X X is countable can be uniquely extended to a Sym ⁡ ( Y ) \Perm(Y) -invariant measure where Y Y is uncountable, and prove that forcing with such measures satisfies the countable chain condition. We also show that we can uniformly distinguish between these random generic structures and the Cohen generic structures that arise from forcing with a strong Fraïssé class: There is a κ \kappa -Borel set of low complexity that contains every Cohen generic structure that is not highly homogeneous but contains no random generic structure, implying that a structure that is not highly homogeneous cannot be both Cohen generic and random generic. Finally, we answer an open question of Kostana in the case of ω 1 \omega_{1} , by establishing a connection between forcing with a strong Fraïssé class and Cohen forcing.

†
†

The present paper is the winner of the Christine Ladd-Franklin Logic Prize for the USA 2025, part of the third edition of the World Logic Prizes Contest.

1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 03C55; Secondary: 03C25, 03E35

1.
Introduction

In 1963,
Cohen
[
Coh63
]
introduced the notion of
forcing
to prove the independence of the continuum hypothesis and the axiom of choice from the axioms of Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory. Forcing enables one to add a new, so-called
generic
, object to an existing
ground
model of set theory.
The method uses a partial order of
conditions
from the ground model, each of which can be thought of as an approximation to a generic object.
In
Cohen forcing
, the conditions are
finite partial functions from
X
to
\{0,1\}
for a set
X
,
and the resulting generic function is a total function from
X
to
\{0,1\}
,
i.e., an element of the generalized Cantor space
2^{X}
.
(In Cohen’s specific method for the independence of the continuum hypothesis,

X=\kappa\times\omega
for a cardinal
\kappa
,
so that the generic function witnesses
that
2^{\omega}\geq\kappa
.)

In the 1970s,
Robinson developed the technique of model-theoretic forcing
(
[
BR70
]
,
[
RBF75
]
,
[
HW75
]
), which has
provided deep insights into model-theoretic algebra and other areas of model theory.
For a language
\Lang
,
this technique allows one to build a generic countably infinite
\Lang
-structure
using conditions that are
finite structures in a
strong Fraïssé class
,
where the underlying set of each condition
is a subset of some common countable set.
Recent work of Golshani
[
Gol19
]
and Kostana
[
Kos23
]
considers
forcing with strong Fraïssé classes
where the underlying set of each condition
is a subset of some common
uncountable
set.

Given a strong Fraïssé class with countably many isomorphism types,
forcing with
it (restricted to structures with underlying set a subset of
\omega
)
produces a countable
Fraïssé limit
, i.e., a structure which is universal for the class and is “maximally symmetric”.
Strong Fraïssé classes appear throughout mathematics. Examples include the class of all finite graphs, whose countable Fraïssé limit is the Rado graph, and the class of all finite linear orders, whose countable Fraïssé limit is the
set of rational numbers as a linear order.

Note that a function from a set
X
to
\{0,1\}
may be viewed as an
\Lang_{1}
-structure with underlying set
X
, where
\Lang_{1}=\{U\}
for a unary relation symbol
U
.
Then Cohen forcing over finite partial functions can be viewed as
the special case of model-theoretic forcing
where the conditions are those elements
of
the strong Fraïssé class of all finite
\Lang_{1}
-structures
whose underlying set is a
subset of
X
.
For this reason, we also
use the term
Cohen forcing
to refer to
forcing with a strong Fraïssé class in an arbitrary relational language,
and we call the generic object it produces a
Cohen generic structure
.

Each comeager Borel subset of
2^{\omega}
can be viewed as
a property that holds of a “typical” element of
2^{\omega}
.
An important characterization of Cohen generic functions from
\omega
to
\{0,1\}
is that they are precisely those elements
of
2^{\omega}

that
are in every comeager
Borel set
that can be described in the ground model.
An alternative view is to consider
each Borel subset of
2^{\omega}
of Lebesgue measure 1 to be
a property that holds of a “typical” element of
2^{\omega}
.
This exemplifies
a more general analogy between category and measure in the study of Cantor space, in which category is concerned with all
possible
configurations, and measure is concerned with configurations that occur
often
.

Under this analogy, the measure-theoretic counterpart
to Cohen forcing is

random forcing
, whose generic functions in
2^{\omega}
are precisely those that
are in every
Borel set of Lebesgue measure
1

that can be described in the ground model.
This notion of forcing was introduced by Solovay in 1964 to show that
there is a model of set theory
in which all subsets of the reals are Lebesgue-measurable
[
Sol70
]
.

Again viewing functions in
2^{\omega}
as
\Lang_{1}
-structures, random forcing can be seen as building an
\Lang_{1}
-structure in a Lebesgue-random generic way. Specifically,
a random generic is
isomorphic to
the
\Lang_{1}
-structure in which
U
holds on an infinite-coinfinite subset of
\omega
.
It is natural to ask how one might
extend random forcing to a forcing notion that
produces a random generic structure in an arbitrary relational language.
To do so, one would need to identify appropriate analogues of Lebesgue measure with which to force.

A key property of Lebesgue probability measure on
2^{\omega}
(equivalently, on the space of
\Lang_{1}
-structures on
\omega
) that makes it suitable for random forcing is that it is
invariant
under permutations of the underlying set
\omega
. This can be thought of as saying that the construction of the generic does not make use of any intrinsic relationship among the elements
of the underlying set (e.g., their ordering).
In this paper, we will propose
that an appropriate extension of random forcing is
forcing with certain
\Perm(X)
-invariant probability measures on the space of
\Lang
-structures, where
X
is an arbitrary set and
\Lang
is an arbitrary relational language.

Invariant probability measures (on spaces of structures)
occur throughout mathematics.
They were studied in the 1930s by
de Finetti
[
dF31
]
in the case of
\Lang_{1}
; these invariant measures are the distributions of
exchangeable sequences
.
The de Finetti representation theorem for invariant probability measures on
\Lang_{1}
-structures
plays a crucial role in the foundations of statistical inference and the philosophical debate between Bayesians and frequentists
[
dF75
]
.

In the 1970s
Aldous
[
Ald81
]
and Hoover
[
Hoo79
]

generalized
de Finetti’s
representation theorem
to invariant probability measures for relational languages of bounded arity.
In the 2000s, work of Lovász, Szegedy, and others
[
Lov12
]

rediscovered a version of this representation theorem in the context of graphs;
the objects representing invariant measures in
this
representation are called

graphons
,
and can be seen as limit objects of convergent sequences of large graphs.
This view of invariant measures provides a new perspective on deep theorems in combinatorics such as Szemerédi’s regularity lemma
[
Sze75
]
.

In the 1960s, Gaifman
[
Gai64
]

studied the model theory of invariant probability measures for arbitrary relational languages,
viewing them as a particular kind of “probabilistic structure”.
The model theory of these probabilistic structures was further developed in recent years by Ackerman, Freer, and Patel.
In particular, many classical results in infinitary logic were shown to have analogues for these probabilistic structures
[
AFP17
]
.

In this paper, we develop
random forcing with invariant measures
, specifically random forcing using an invariant probability
\kappa
-measure
on the space of
\Lang
-structures with a fixed underlying set, where
\Lang
is an arbitrary relational language and
\kappa
is an infinite ordinal.
We call the generic structures produced by these forcing notions
random generic structures
.
We investigate the relationship between random generic structures and the invariant measures used to create them.
This
work can be viewed
as studying the connection between a “random sample” from a probabilistic structure and the probabilistic structure itself.
We also study the connections between random generic structures and Cohen generic structures.

Further, we
provide a robust tool for lifting measure-theoretic properties of invariant measures from Cantor space (
2^{\omega}
) to generalized Cantor space (
2^{\kappa}
).
In addition, we show a way to generalize random forcing to produce uncountable as well as countable structures.
Our results provide a comprehensive theory of forcing using invariant measures,
simultaneously generalizing random forcing with Lebesgue measure and model-theoretic forcing with strong Fraïssé classes.

1.1.
Outline of the Paper

In
Section

2
, we review some of the ideas that will be needed for our work, including forcing, Boolean algebras, the logic action,
\kappa
-Borel codes, the almost-sure theory of a measure, invariant probability
\kappa
-measures, and Fraïssé limits. The notion of a
\kappa
-Borel code
is of special importance, as we will consider relativizations of measures to larger models of set theory, and
\kappa
-Borel codes give us an absolute way to describe them.

In
Section

3
, we review some of the key properties of
Cohen forcing over arbitrary sets as well as of random forcing with Lebesgue measure over arbitrary sets.
Because functions from a set
X
to
\{0,1\}
are cryptomorphic to
\Lang_{1}
-structures on
X
,
the results in this section
can be respectively viewed as a special case of Cohen forcing over arbitrary structures and of random forcing with respect to an appropriate invariant measure over arbitrary structures,
which
we study in
Sections

4
and
6
.

In
Section

4
, we review Cohen forcings over strong Fraïssé classes and consider the relationship between generics for
different strong Fraïssé classes. For an infinite set
X
, we show that forcing over
X
using any non-trivial strong Fraïssé class with at most
|X|
-many isomorphism types will yield a model of set theory containing a Cohen generic function in
2^{X}
. We also show that when
|X|\leq\omega_{1}
such forcings are equivalent to Cohen forcing over
X
, in the sense that they yield the same forcing extensions. This answers an open question from
[
Kos23
]
in the case of
\omega_{1}
.

In
Section

5
,
we turn our focus to invariant probability
\kappa
-measures on the space of structures with a given infinite underlying set.
In
Section

5.1
,
we show that every such invariant measure has a relativization to every model of set theory. We also show that for all infinite sets
X
and
Y
, any
\Perm(Y)
-invariant measure
can be uniquely lifted to an analogous
\Perm(X)
-invariant measure.
In
Section

5.2
, we
consider three key properties that an invariant measure may satisfy, namely
being ergodic, extreme, or dissociated.
A fundamental result in probability theory is that these notions are equivalent for invariant probability measures on the space of structures with a countable underlying set.
We show that these three notions
are each absolute between models of set theory.
We then use this fact along with our lifting technique from
Section

5.1

to show that the three notions coincide for invariant probability
\kappa
-measures on the space of structures with an arbitrary uncountable underlying set.

In
Section

6
, we develop the notion of forcing with an invariant probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
to obtain a
\mu
-random generic structure. We show that forcing with such an invariant measure has the countable chain condition, and hence preserves all cardinals and cofinalities.
We also show that for every
\mu
-generic filter there is a unique
\mu
-random generic structure that characterizes the filter. This
\mu
-random generic structure can be thought of as a “random sample” from the invariant measure, and we study the relationship between such a sample and the measure it came from. Specifically, when
\mu
is ergodic, the almost-sure theory of the measure is complete, and we show that every
\mu
-random generic structure
must satisfy the almost-sure theory of
\mu
.
This holds even when
\mu
is
properly ergodic
, in which case the almost-sure theory has no models in the ground model of set theory by
[
AFKP17a
]
.

In
Section

7
, we consider how much information can be recovered about an invariant probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
given access only to a random generic structure for
\mu
. We show that when
\mu
is ergodic, one can completely recover
\mu
from any random generic structure for
\mu
.
Next we study the relationship between Cohen generic structures and random generic structures.
Certain Fraïssé classes have the property of being
highly homogeneous
.
We show that for any infinite set
X
, there is a single
|X|
-Borel code such that for every Cohen generic structure for a strong Fraïssé class that is not highly homogeneous,
the Cohen generic structure
is contained in the set represented by the code. However, for any invariant probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
,
every
random generic structure for
\mu
is contained in the complement of the set represented by the code. As a consequence,
if a structure is not highly homogeneous, it
cannot be simultaneously Cohen generic and random generic.

Finally, in
Section

8
, we conclude with several natural conjectures.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

We work in an unnamed but fixed background model of
ZFC
. We will use
X
,
Y
,
Z
and their variants (e.g.,
X_{0}
,
X_{1}
, or
X^{*}
) to represent sets in this background model of set theory, and
X
and
Y
will always be infinite. We write
\CARD
to denote the collection of cardinals. For
n\in\omega
, we define
[n]=\{0,\dots,n-1\}
. We write
2^{Z}
to denote the set of functions from
Z
to
\{0,1\}
. We write
\mathcal{P}(Z)
to denote the powerset of
Z
(i.e., the collection of subsets of
Z
). We write
\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(Z)
to denote the collection of finite subsets of
Z
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\omega}(Z)
to denote the collection of countably infinite subsets of
Z
. The symbol
\kappa
will denote a fixed infinite ordinal,
although intuitively it may be helpful to think of
\kappa
as a cardinal (but when we move to a larger model of set theory, we do not require
\kappa
to stay a cardinal).

We write
\langle a_{i}\rangle_{i\in Z}
to denote a sequence indexed by a set
Z
, and write

\langle a_{i}\rangle_{i\in Z}\subseteq B
to mean that
a_{i}\in B
for
i\in Z
.
For a sequence
\langle r_{i}\rangle_{i\in Z}\subseteq[0,\infty)
, we define
\sum_{i\in Z}r_{i}=\sup\bigl\{\sum_{i\in Z_{0}}r_{i}\,:\,Z_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(Z)\bigr\}
. For
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
\lfloor r\rfloor
be the largest integer with
\lfloor r\rfloor\leq r
.
Given a finite tuple
{\bm{a}}
(of variables or elements of some set), we write
|{\bm{a}}|
to denote its length.

We write
Z_{0}\triangle Z_{1}
for the symmetric difference of
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
, i.e.,
(Z_{0}\setminus Z_{1})\cup(Z_{1}\setminus Z_{0})
.
When
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
Z
and
a\in Z
, we write
[a]_{\sim}
for the
\sim
-equivalence class of
a
. We let
\Perm(Z)
be the group of permutations of
Z
, and for
n\in\omega
we write
\Perm(n)
to mean
\Perm([n])
. We write
\Perm^{<\omega}(Z)
for the group of permutations of
Z
with finite support, i.e., the subgroup consisting of those
g\in\Perm(Z)
for which
\{a\in Z\,:\,g(a)\neq a\}
is finite.

The
language of set theory
is
\Lang_{\in}=\{\in\}
where
\in
is a binary relation symbol. By a
model of set theory
we mean a transitive model of
ZFC

that
is a subset of our background model of
ZFC
. We will use
V
and its variants to represent models of set theory. For a formula
\varphi
in set theory, we let
\varphi^{V}
be the collection of elements
a\in V
such that
V\models\varphi(a)
.
(We will sometimes elide mention of
V
when the model of set theory is clear from context.)

All languages will be relational unless we explicitly say otherwise. We will use
\Lang
and its variants
to represent languages.
We will use calligraphic letters for structures in some language and the corresponding Roman letter for their underlying set (e.g.,
{\mathcal{M}}
is a structure with underlying set
M
).
Suppose
{\mathcal{M}}
is an
\Lang
-structure; when

\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang
and
M_{0}\subseteq M
, we write
{\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{M_{0}}
to denote the substructure of
{\mathcal{M}}
with underlying set
M_{0}
, we write
{\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{\Lang_{0}}
to denote the reduct of
{\mathcal{M}}
to an
\Lang_{0}
-structure, and we write
{\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{\Lang_{0},M_{0}}
to denote
({\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{\Lang_{0}})\!\restriction_{M_{0}}
.
When
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
are two
\Lang
-structures, we write
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}
to mean that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a substructure of
{\mathcal{B}}
. We write
{\mathrm{{Aut}}}({\mathcal{M}})
to denote the automorphism group of a structure
{\mathcal{M}}
.

For an infinite

\kappa\in\CARD
, we let
{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,\omega}}(\Lang)
be the smallest collection of formulas containing all atomic
\Lang
-formulas and closed under (1) variable substitutions, (2) adding a new free variable, (3) negations, (4) existential and universal quantification by a single variable, and (5) conjunctions and disjunctions of size
<\kappa
where all formulas have free variables among a common finite set.
We let
\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\omega}(\Lang)=\bigcup_{\kappa\in\CARD}{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,\omega}}(\Lang)
. If
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\omega}(\Lang)
, we let
\arity(\varphi)
denote the
arity
of
\varphi
, i.e., the number of free variables in
\varphi
. If
\varphi\in{\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,\omega}}(\Lang)
is a sentence (i.e., has arity
0
), we write
\models\varphi
when
\varphi
is true in all
\Lang
-structures. For a formula
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\omega}(\Lang)
and
\Lang
-structure
{\mathcal{M}}
, we let
\varphi^{\mathcal{M}}=\{{\bm{a}}\in M^{k}\,:\,{\mathcal{M}}\models\varphi({\bm{a}})\}
, where
k
is the arity of
\varphi
.

We call a tuple
{\bm{x}}

non-redundant
if it is injective, considered as a function. For
k\in\omega
, we let
Z^{(k)}
be the collection of non-redundant tuples of elements of
Z
of size
k
. We let
\mathfrak{NR}(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1})
be the first-order formula
\bigwedge_{0\leq i<j<k}(x_{i}\neq x_{j})
, and say that a formula
\varphi
of arity
k
is
non-redundant
when
\models(\forall x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1})\,\bigl(\varphi(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1})\rightarrow\mathfrak{NR}(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1})\bigr)
, i.e.,
\varphi
holds only of non-redundant tuples. An
\Lang
-structure
{\mathcal{M}}
is
non-redundant
when for all relation symbols
R
in
\Lang
of arity
k
, we have

{\mathcal{M}}\models(\forall x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1})\,\bigl(R(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1})\rightarrow\mathfrak{NR}(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1})\bigr).

By an
\Lang
-literal
we mean a quantifier-free
\Lang
-formula
built from atomic
\Lang
-formulas using only negation.

We say that
{\mathcal{Z}}=(Z,{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z))

is a
\kappa
-measurable space
when

•

\emptyset\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Z)
,

•

{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z)
is closed under complements, and

•

{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z)
is closed under unions of size
\leq\kappa
.

For
\kappa\in\CARD
, we let
\sigma_{\kappa}(2^{Z})
be the smallest subset of
\mathcal{P}(2^{Z})
closed under complements, unions of size
\leq\kappa
,
and containing all sets of the form
\{f\in 2^{Z}\,:\,p\subseteq f\}
where
p\in 2^{Z_{0}}
for some
Z_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(Z)
.

We say that a probability
measure
\mu
on a
\kappa
-measure space
(Z,{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z))
is a
probability
\kappa
-measure
if,
whenever

\{A_{i}\}_{i\in\kappa}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z)
consists of pairwise disjoint sets, we have

\mu(\bigcup_{i\in\kappa}A_{i})=\sum_{i\in\kappa}\mu(A_{i})
. Note that a probability
\omega
-measure is simply a probability measure.

Suppose
(Z_{0},{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z_{0}))
and
(Z_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z_{1}))
are
\kappa
-measurable spaces and
\alpha\colon Z_{0}\to Z_{1}
is a measurable function, i.e.,
\alpha^{-1}(A)\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z_{0})
for all
A\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z_{1})
. For a probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
on
(Z_{0},{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z_{0}))
, the
pushforward of
\mu
along
\alpha

is the probability
\kappa
-measure

\alpha^{*}(\mu)

on
(Z_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z_{1}))
defined by

\alpha^{*}(\mu)(A)=\mu(\alpha^{-1}(A))

for
A\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(Z_{1})
.

2.2.
Forcing

Our forcing notation
and terminology
will follow that of
[
Kun80
]
. Specifically, when
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
is a partial order, we will interpret
p\leq_{\mathbb{P}}q

to mean
that “
p
is stronger than
q
”. By a
filter
on
\mathbb{P}
we mean a nonempty set
F\subseteq P
such that

•

if
p\leq_{\mathbb{P}}q
and
p\in F
then
q\in F
, and

•

if
p,q\in F
then there is an
r\in F
such that
r\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p
and
r\leq_{\mathbb{P}}q
.

When the meaning is clear from context, we will write
\leq
rather than
\leq_{\mathbb{P}}
and elide the distinction between
\mathbb{P}
and
P
.

A filter
F
is
generic
over
V
(for
\mathbb{P}
) when, for all dense
D\subseteq P
with
D\in V
, we have
F\cap D\neq\emptyset
. (We will typically use some variant of
\mathbb{G}
or
\mathbb{H}
to denote a generic filter.)
If
\varphi
is a formula in the language of set theory of arity
k
and
a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1}
are
\mathbb{P}
-names and
p\in P
,
we write
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1})
when
p
forces
\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1})
.

If
\mathbb{Q}=(Q,\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
is a pre-order, we
let
x\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}y

denote
the
relation
(x\leq_{\mathbb{Q}}y)\wedge(y\leq_{\mathbb{Q}}x)
. We
let
\mathbb{Q}/\!\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}=(Q/\!\sim_{\mathbb{Q}},\,\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
be the corresponding partial order and
\iota_{\mathbb{Q}}\colon\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{Q}/\!\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}
be the map where
\iota_{\mathbb{Q}}(a)=[a]_{\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}}
for all
a\in Q
. Note that (as in
[
Kun80
]
) forcing can be developed for pre-orders in an obvious way. For every
\mathbb{Q}
-name
a
we can find a
\mathbb{Q}/\!\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}
-name
\iota_{\mathbb{Q}}(a)
which is obtained by hereditarily replacing each element
q\in Q
with an equivalence class
[q]_{\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Then for any
q\in Q
, any formula
\varphi
in the language of set theory of arity
k
and any
\mathbb{Q}
-names
a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1}
, we have

q\Vdash_{\mathbb{Q}}\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1})\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad[q]_{\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}}\Vdash_{\mathbb{Q}/\!\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}}\varphi(\iota_{\mathbb{Q}}(a_{0}),\dots,\iota_{\mathbb{Q}}(a_{k-1})).

Definition 2.1
.

A forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
is

Definition 1
.

non-trivial when every condition in
\mathbb{P}
has two incompatible extensions.

2.3.
Boolean Algebras

Here we recall basic information and definitions regarding Boolean algebras.

Definition 2.2
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
are Boolean algebras with
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}
. We say that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a

Definition 2
.

regular subalgebra
of
{\mathcal{B}}

if whenever
A_{0}\subseteq A
and
a=(\sup A_{0})^{{\mathcal{A}}}
(i.e.,
a
is the supremum of
A_{0}
in
{\mathcal{A}}
), then
a=(\sup A_{0})^{{\mathcal{B}}}
(i.e.,
a
is the supremum of
A_{0}
in
{\mathcal{B}}
).

Definition 2.3
.

Let
Z
be a collection of sets. We say that a subset
C\subseteq Z
is

Definition 3
.

unbounded if
for every
z\in Z
there is some
c\in C
such that
z\subseteq c
.
The set
C
is

Definition 4
.

closed if whenever
C_{0}\subseteq C
is such that
\bigcup C_{0}\in Z
, then also
\bigcup C_{0}\in C
.
The set
C
is

Definition 5
.

club if it is closed and unbounded.

Definition 2.4
.

The

Definition 6
.

density of a Boolean algebra
{\mathcal{B}}
is the least size of a dense subset
of
{\mathcal{B}}
.
We say that
{\mathcal{B}}
has

Definition 7
.

uniform density when for all
b\in B
, the Boolean algebras
{\mathcal{B}}
and

{\mathcal{B}}\!\restriction_{\{b^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}\,:\,b^{\prime}\leq b\}}

have the same density.

Definition 2.5
.

Given a non-trivial forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
, we write
\RO(\mathbb{P})
to denote the unique Boolean completion of
\mathbb{P}
.

Note that there is an embedding
i\colon\mathbb{P}\to\RO(\mathbb{P})\setminus\{0\}
whose image is dense.

Definition 2.6
.

Forcing notions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are

Definition 8
.

forcing isomorphic, written
\mathbb{P}\approx\mathbb{Q}
, when
\RO(\mathbb{P})\cong\RO(\mathbb{Q})
.

If
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are forcing isomorphic, then they give the same forcing extensions of the universe in the sense that for any generic filter
\mathbb{G}

that is
\mathbb{P}
-generic over
V
, there is a generic filter
\mathbb{H}

which is
\mathbb{Q}
-generic over
V
with
V[\mathbb{G}]=V[\mathbb{H}]
, and vice-versa.

2.4.
Logic Action

We now introduce the logic action for a set
Z
. This is an action of
\Perm(Z)
on the space of
\Lang
-structures with underlying set
Z
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z)
be the collection of
\Lang
-structures with underlying set
Z
. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\omega}(\Lang)
of arity
n
and
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in Z
we let

{\llbracket\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\rrbracket}_{Z}^{\Lang}=\{{\mathcal{M}}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z)\,:\,{\mathcal{M}}\models\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\}.

We will simply write
{\llbracket\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\rrbracket}_{Z}
when the language
\Lang
is clear from context.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z))
be the smallest
subset of
\mathcal{P}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z))

that is closed under unions of size
\leq\kappa
, closed under complements, and which contains all sets of the form
{\llbracket\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\rrbracket}_{Z}^{\Lang}
, where
\varphi
is a
\Lang
-literal of arity
n
and
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in Z
. We write
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Z)
to denote the
\kappa
-measurable space
\bigl(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z),\,\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z))\bigr)
.

Note that
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Z_{0})
is isomorphic to
the generalized Cantor space
(2^{Z_{1}},\sigma_{\kappa}(2^{Z_{1}}))
for some set
Z_{1}
.

Definition 2.9
.

For any
f\in 2^{\coprod_{R\in\Lang}\,Z^{\arity(R)}}
,
let
\iota_{\Lang,Z}(f)
be the
\Lang
-structure with underlying set
Z
such that for
R\in\Lang
and
a_{0},\dots,a_{\arity(R)-1}\in Z
,
we have

\iota_{\Lang,Z}(f)\models R(a_{0},\dots,a_{\arity(R)-1})\text{~~if and only if~~}f(R,\langle a_{0},\dots,a_{\arity(R)-1}\rangle)=1,

where
f(R,\langle a_{0},\dots,a_{\arity(R)-1}\rangle)
is the evaluation of
f
at the obvious
summand.

The following is immediate.

Lemma 2.10
.

The map
\iota_{\Lang,Z}
is an isomorphism between

(2^{\coprod_{R\in\Lang}\,Z^{\arity(R)}},\ \sigma_{\kappa}(2^{\coprod_{R\in\Lang}\,Z^{\arity(R)}}))

and
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Z)
as
\kappa
-measurable spaces.

We now define the logic action.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\circ_{\Lang,Z}\colon\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z)\times\Perm(Z)\to\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z)
be the map where, for
g\in\Perm(Z)
, for
{\mathcal{M}}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z)
, for
R\in\Lang
of arity
n
, and for
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in Z
, we have

{\mathcal{M}}\models R(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\text{~~if and only if~~}\circ_{\Lang,Z}({\mathcal{M}},g)\models R(g(a_{0}),\dots,g(a_{n-1})).

We call this map the

Definition 9
.

logic action on
\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z)
.
For
g\in\Perm(Z)
,
when no confusion can occur,
we will abuse notation and also write
g
to refer to the map
\circ_{\Lang,Z}(\,\cdot\,,g)
.

Note that whenever
g\in\Perm(Z)
and
A\in\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z))
we have
g``[A]\in\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z))
as well, and in particular
\circ_{\Lang,Z}(\,\cdot\,,g)
is a measurable map.

2.5.
Borel Codes

We now formalize the notion of
Borel codes
.

Definition 2.12
.

The collection of

Definition 10
.

\kappa
-Borel codes over
\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z)
, denoted
\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}
, is defined to be the smallest set satisfying the following.

•

\langle 0,\varphi,\langle a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\rangle\rangle\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}

for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\omega}(\Lang)
of arity
n
that is a finite conjunction of literals and every
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in Z
.

•

\langle 1,\neg,\gamma\rangle\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}

for every
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}
.
We will use
\neg\gamma
to denote
\langle 1,\neg,\gamma\rangle
.

•

\langle 2,\bigvee,\Gamma\rangle,\langle 3,\bigwedge,\Gamma\rangle\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}

for every
\Gamma=\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in\kappa}\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}
.
We will use
\bigvee\Gamma
to denote
\langle 2,\bigvee,\Gamma\rangle
and
\bigwedge\Gamma
to denote
\langle 3,\bigwedge,\Gamma\rangle
. When
\Gamma=\{\zeta,\gamma\}
, we will use
\zeta\wedge\gamma
to represent
\bigwedge\{\zeta,\gamma\}
and
\zeta\vee\gamma
to represent
\bigvee\{\zeta,\gamma\}
.

When
\kappa=\omega
we will simply write
\mathbb{B}_{\Lang:Z}
. The

Definition 11
.

rank of a Borel code
\gamma
, denoted
\rank(\gamma)
, is defined as follows.

•

If
\gamma=\langle 0,\varphi,\langle a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\rangle\rangle
then
\rank(\gamma)=0
.

•

If
\gamma=\neg\zeta
then
\rank(\gamma)=\rank(\zeta)+1
.

•

If
\gamma=\bigwedge\Gamma
or
\bigvee\Gamma
then
\rank(\gamma)=\sup\{\rank(\zeta)+1\,:\,\zeta\in\Gamma\}
.

Definition 2.13
.

We define the

Definition 12
.

interpretation of
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}
, denoted
\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

For a probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Z)
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}
, we say that the

Definition 13
.

value of
\mu
on
\gamma
, written

\mu(\gamma)
, is
\mu(\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.58865pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

.

Note that
\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(Z))=\{\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

.
We now describe two important collections of Borel codes.

Definition 2.14
.

The
\kappa
-Borel codes of rank
0
are called

Definition 14
.

basic, and we write
\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:Z}
to denote the collection of basic
\kappa
-Borel codes.
A
\kappa
-Borel code is

Definition 15
.

almost basic when it is of the form
\bigvee\Gamma
where
\Gamma
is finite
and every element of
\Gamma
is a basic
\kappa
-Borel code; we write
\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:Z}
to denote the collection of almost basic
\kappa
-Borel codes.

As we will see in
Proposition

2.18
, the values a probability
\kappa
-measure takes on the basic
\kappa
-Borel codes
suffice to pin down the measure uniquely.
The interpretations
of almost basic
\kappa
-Borel codes
correspond
to the realizations of quantifier-free first-order formulas. Hence the collection of almost basic
\kappa
-Borel codes is the same in any model of set theory (that contains
the underlying language and the underlying set).

The following is one of the main motivations for considering
probability
\kappa
-measures and
\kappa
-Borel codes
for
\kappa>\omega
,
as opposed to simply considering
probability
\omega
-measures and
\omega
-Borel codes.

Lemma 2.15
.

Suppose
Z,\Lang\in V

and let

\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)^{V}
be a formula.
Suppose that either

•

V\models|Z|\leq\kappa
, or

•

\varphi
is quantifier-free.

Then for every tuple
{\bm{a}}\subseteq Z

there is some code

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi({\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}
such that in any model of set theory
V^{*}
with
V\subseteq V^{*}
, we have

V^{*}\models{\llbracket\varphi({\bm{a}})\rrbracket}_{Z}^{\Lang}=\mathord{\hbox to36.2pt{\vbox to13.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.10187pt\lower-5.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

Proof 2.16
.

If
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\omega}(\Lang)
is a literal of arity
n
and

a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in Z
, define

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\langle 0,\varphi,\langle a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\rangle\rangle
.

Let
F\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
be the collection of all
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
-formulas that satisfy the conclusion for all instantiations by tuples
from
Z
of appropriate length. If

\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
satisfies

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:Z}

for all
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in Z
then it is immediate that
\varphi\in F
.

Let
\varphi({\bm{x}})\in F
where
{\bm{x}}
consists of distinct variables, let
f\colon{\bm{x}}\to{\bm{z}}
be a map, and let
\psi({\bm{z}})=\varphi({\bm{x}}/f)
, i.e., the result of substituting the corresponding variables of
{\bm{z}}
in place of variables of
{\bm{x}}
. Then letting
{\bm{a}}
be a tuple of elements of
Z
of length
|{\bm{z}}|
(formally, a mapping that assigns each variable in
{\bm{z}}
an element of
Z
),
we can take
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\psi({\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(f\circ{\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
. Hence
\psi({\bm{z}})\in F
as well.

If
\varphi\in F
then we can take
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\neg\varphi\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\neg\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
. Similarly, if
\Phi=\{\psi_{i}\}_{i\in\kappa}\subseteq F
we can take
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bigvee_{i\in I}\Phi\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\bigvee_{i\in\kappa}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\psi_{i}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
and
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bigwedge_{i\in I}\Phi\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\bigwedge_{i\in\kappa}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\psi_{i}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
. Therefore
F
is closed under
\kappa
-sized Boolean operations. Hence
F
contains all quantifier-free formulas.

Now suppose
V\models|Z|\leq\kappa
. Further suppose
\varphi=(\exists y)\psi({\bm{x}},y)
. We can take
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\bigvee_{a\in Z}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\psi({\bm{x}},a)\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
. Note that
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}
as
|Z|\leq\kappa
. Finally, if
\varphi=(\forall y)\psi({\bm{x}},y)
we can take
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\neg(\exists y)\neg\psi({\bm{x}},y)\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
.

Therefore
F
is closed under variable substitution,
\kappa
-sized Boolean operations, and single-variable existential and universal quantification. Hence, if
V\models|Z|\leq\kappa
, then
F=\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
.

Lemma

2.15
motivates the following definition.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
and let
\mu
be a probability
\kappa
-measure
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Z)
. Suppose
{\bm{a}}\subseteq Z
is a tuple whose length is the arity of
\varphi
. Whenever either
\varphi
is quantifier-free or
|Z|\leq\kappa
, then we define
\mu(\varphi({\bm{a}}))=\mu(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi({\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})
.
For
|Z|\leq\kappa
,
we define
\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)
to be the collection of sentences
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
such that
\mu(\varphi)=1
. We call
\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)
the

Definition 16
.

almost-sure theory of
\mu
.

The following
provides
a useful way to construct probability
\kappa
-measures on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Z)
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Suppose
\mu^{\circ}\colon\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:Z}\to[0,1]

satisfies the following conditions.

•

\mu(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bigwedge\emptyset\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})=1
.

•

If
\varphi
and
\psi
are finite conjunctions of
\Lang
-literals
and
\models\varphi({\bm{x}})\rightarrow\psi({\bm{x}})
then for any
{\bm{a}}\in Z
,
we have

\mu^{\circ}(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\psi({\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})\geq\mu^{\circ}(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi({\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})
.

•

For any
\Lang
-literal
\psi({\bm{x}})
and
{\bm{a}}\in Z
,
we have

\mu(\psi({\bm{a}})\wedge\neg\psi({\bm{a}}))=0
.

•

For all tuples
{\bm{a}}\in Z
and all
\Lang
-literals
\langle\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\rangle_{i\in[n+1]}
,
we have

\mu^{\circ}\bigg(\bigwedge_{i\in[n]}\psi_{i}({\bm{a}})\bigg)=\mu^{\circ}\bigg(\psi_{n}({\bm{a}})\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in[n]}\psi_{i}({\bm{a}})\bigg)+\mu^{\circ}\bigg(\neg\psi_{n}({\bm{a}})\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in[n]}\psi_{i}({\bm{a}})\bigg).

Then there is a unique probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Z)
such that for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:Z}
, we have

\mu(\gamma)=\mu^{\circ}(\gamma).

Proof 2.19
.

This follows from the
\kappa
-measurable version of the Carathéodory extension theorem.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\mu
be a probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Z)
. For
\gamma,\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}
, we write
\gamma\preceq_{\mu}\zeta
when
\mu(\gamma\wedge\neg\zeta)=0
, and
we write
\gamma\sim_{\mu}\zeta
when both
\gamma\preceq_{\mu}\zeta
and
\zeta\preceq_{\mu}\gamma
hold.
Define

{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)=\bigl(\{\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z}\,:\,\mu(\gamma)>0\},\,\preceq_{\mu}\bigr).

It is straightforward to check that
\preceq_{\mu}
is a pre-order
and that
\sim_{\mu}
is an equivalence relation.

We will use the following to transfer

\kappa
-Borel codes across injections.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
i\colon Z_{0}\to Z_{1}
be an injection.
For
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z_{0}}
,
define
i[\gamma]\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Z_{1}}

by induction as follows.

•

i[\langle 0,\varphi,\langle a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\rangle\rangle]=\langle 0,\varphi,\langle i(a_{0}),\dots,i(a_{n-1})\rangle\rangle
.

•

i[\neg\varphi]=\neg i[\varphi]
.

•

i[\bigwedge\Gamma]=\bigwedge\{i[\gamma]\,:\,\gamma\in\Gamma\}
.

•

i[\bigvee\Gamma]=\bigvee\{i[\gamma]\,:\,\gamma\in\Gamma\}
.

2.6.
Invariant Measures

We now review the definition of an invariant probability
\kappa
-measure and the associated notions of dissociatedness, extremality, and ergodicity.
Recall that
X
is an infinite set.

Definition 2.22
.

A probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
is

Definition 17
.

invariant if for all
g\in\Perm(X)
and all

A\in\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(X))
, we have

\mu(A)=\mu(g``[A]).

For the rest of this paper, we will use the term
invariant measure
to mean an invariant probability
\kappa
-measure.

We will often be interested in invariant measures that exhibit certain key properties,
namely being
dissociated
,

extreme
,
or

ergodic
.
In the case where
|X|=|\Lang|=|\kappa|=\omega
, these three notions are known to be equivalent,
as we state in Proposition
2.27
.
In
Theorem

5.38
,
we will show that they are equivalent even without this cardinality constraint.

Definition 2.23
.

A probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
is

Definition 18
.

dissociated
if for all disjoint finite
X_{0},X_{1}\subseteq X
, all
\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X_{0}}
, and all
\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X_{1}}
,
we have

\mu(\gamma_{0}\cap\gamma_{1})=\mu(\gamma_{0})\cdot\mu(\gamma_{1}).

In other words,
\kappa
-Borel codes that only refer to disjoint finite index sets are
\mu
-independent.

Definition 2.24
.

An invariant probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu

on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
is

Definition 19
.

extreme if, whenever
\nu
and
\eta
are distinct invariant probability
\kappa
-measures on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
with
\mu=r\cdot\nu+s\cdot\eta
, then either
r=0
or
s=0
.

In other words, an invariant measure is extreme if it is not
a
convex combination of two distinct invariant measures.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
\mu
be an invariant probability
\kappa
-measure
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
and let
A\in\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(X))
. The set
A
is

Definition 20
.

\mu
-almost invariant if for all
g\in\Perm(X)
, we have
\mu(g``[A]\triangle A)=0
. For
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
we say that
\gamma
is

Definition 21
.

\mu
-almost invariant when
\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.58865pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.97 7.58 C -2.57 7.58 -2.37 8.76 -1.98 8.76 C -1.7 8.76 -1.45 8.18 -1.19 7.58 C -0.94 6.97 -0.69 6.4 -0.41 6.4 C -0.12 6.4 0.12 6.97 0.38 7.58 C 0.64 8.18 0.88 8.76 1.17 8.76 L 2.97 7.58}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
is.

Definition 2.26
.

An invariant probability
\kappa
-measure

\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
is

Definition 22
.

ergodic if, whenever
A\in\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(X))
is
\mu
-almost invariant, we have
\mu(A)\in\{0,1\}
.

The next proposition states that in the countable case, the notions of dissociatedness, extremality, and ergodicity all agree for invariant probability measures.

Proposition 2.27
.

Suppose
|X|=|\Lang|=\omega
and let
\mu
be an invariant probability measure on
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(X)
. The following are equivalent.

•

\mu
is dissociated.

•

\mu
is extreme.

•

\mu
is ergodic.

Proof 2.28
.

See
[
Kal05
, Lemma 7.35]

as well as
[
Ack21
, Corollary 2.46]
.

One of the reasons why ergodic invariant probability measures are so important is that, in the countable context,
every invariant probability measure is a mixture of ergodic ones.

Proposition 2.29

(
[
Kal05
, Theorem A1.3]
)
.

Suppose
|X|=|\Lang|=\omega
and let
\mu
be an invariant probability measure on
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(X)
. Then
\mu
is a mixture
of ergodic invariant probability measures on
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(X)
.

2.7.
Fraïssé Theory

We now recall some basic notions of Fraïssé theory. For more on this, see
[
Hod93
, Chapter 7.1]
.

Definition 2.30
.

Suppose
\bm{K}
is a collection of finite
\Lang
-structures that is closed under isomorphism.

We say that
\bm{K}
has the

Definition 23
.

hereditary property (HP) if, whenever
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\in\bm{K}
and
M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}
,
we have
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\!\restriction_{M_{0}}\in\bm{K}
.

We say that

\bm{K}
has the

Definition 24
.

joint embedding property (JEP) if, whenever
{\mathcal{M}}_{0},{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\in\bm{K}
, there exist an
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}\in\bm{K}
and embeddings
i\colon{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
and
j\colon{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
.

We say that

\bm{K}
has the

Definition 25
.

amalgamation property (AP) if, whenever
{\mathcal{M}}_{0},{\mathcal{M}}_{1},{\mathcal{M}}_{2}\in\bm{K}
and the maps
i_{1}\colon{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
i_{2}\colon{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
are embeddings,
there
exist
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}\in\bm{K}
and embeddings
j_{1}\colon{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{3}
and
j_{2}\colon{\mathcal{M}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{3}
such that
j_{1}\circ i_{1}=j_{2}\circ i_{2}
.

If, further,
j_{1},j_{2}
can be chosen such that

j_{1}``[{\mathcal{M}}_{1}]\cap j_{2}``[{\mathcal{M}}_{2}]=(j_{1}\circ i_{1})``[{\mathcal{M}}_{0}],

then we say that
\bm{K}
has the

Definition 26
.

strong amalgamation property (SAP).

We say that
\bm{K}
is a

Definition 27
.

Fraïssé class (in
\Lang
) when it satisfies (HP), (JEP), and (AP). A

Definition 28
.

strong Fraïssé class is a Fraïssé class that satisfies (SAP).

Definition 2.31
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be an
\Lang
-structure.
The

Definition 29
.

age of
{\mathcal{M}}
is the collection of those
finite
\Lang
-structures that embed into
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Definition 2.32
.

Suppose
\bm{K}
is a Fraïssé class in
\Lang
. A

Definition 30
.

Fraïssé limit
of
\bm{K}
is an
\Lang
-structure
{\mathcal{M}}
such that

•

for every
{\mathcal{N}}\in\bm{K}
there is an embedding from
{\mathcal{N}}
into
{\mathcal{M}}
, and

•

if
{\mathcal{N}}\in\bm{K}
and
i,j\colon{\mathcal{N}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
are embeddings then there is a
g\in{\mathrm{{Aut}}}({\mathcal{M}})

such that
i=g\circ j
.

The following is standard.

Proposition 2.33
.

Let
\bm{K}
be a collection of finite
\Lang
-structures that is closed under isomorphism.
Suppose
\bm{K}
has only countably many isomorphism types.
Then we have the following.

•

\bm{K}
is the age of an
\Lang
-structure if and only if
\bm{K}
has (HP) and (JEP).

•

\bm{K}
has a countably infinite Fraïssé limit
if and only if
\bm{K}
has (HP), (JEP), and (AP), i.e.,
\bm{K}
is a Fraïssé class.

Definition 2.34
.

A strong Fraïssé class

\bm{K}
in
\Lang
is

Definition 31
.

trivial if for all literals
\psi
in
\Lang
of arity
n
, either (i)
for all
{\mathcal{A}}\in\bm{K}
, we have

{\mathcal{A}}\models(\forall x_{0},\dots x_{n-1})\,\bigl(\mathfrak{NR}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\rightarrow\psi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\bigr),

or (ii)
for all
{\mathcal{A}}\in\bm{K}
, we have

{\mathcal{A}}\models(\forall x_{0},\dots x_{n-1})\,\bigl(\mathfrak{NR}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\rightarrow\neg\psi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\bigr).

A structure is

Definition 32
.

trivial when its age is a trivial strong Fraïssé class.

Remark 2.35
.

For the rest of the paper, unless we explicitly mention otherwise, we will assume that all strong Fraïssé classes are non-trivial and that all infinite structures
are non-trivial.

3.
Cohen Forcing and Random Forcing on
2^{X}

In this section, we review properties of Cohen forcings and random forcings (with Lebesgue measure) over arbitrary sets.
Both of these can be seen as forcing with specific subsets of
2^{X}
.

Let
\Lang_{1}
be the language with a single unary relation symbol
U_{1}
. By
Lemma

2.10
, there is an isomorphism
\iota_{\Lang_{1},X}
from
(2^{X},\sigma_{\kappa}(2^{X}))
to
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{1}}(X)
.
In general, we will not distinguish between
2^{X}
and
\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{1}}(X)
and we will abuse notation in the obvious way by referring to a
\kappa
-Borel code in
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{1}}(X)
as a
\kappa
-Borel code in
2^{X}
, and likewise abuse notation by referring to the interpretation of such a
\kappa
-Borel code as a subset of
2^{X}
.

Since
2^{X}
and
\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{1}}(X)
are isomorphic,
Cohen forcing and random forcing with Lebesgue measure can also be thought of as forcing with subsets of
\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{1}}(X)
. Hence Cohen forcings and random forcings with Lebesgue measure on
2^{X}

are special cases, respectively, of forcing with a strong Fraïssé class and forcing with an invariant measure on the space of structures with a given underlying set
X
.

3.1.
Cohen Forcing

The partial order for Cohen forcing over a set
X
, denoted
\mathbb{C}[X]
, is defined to be

\mathbb{C}[X]=\bigl\{p\,:\,(\exists X_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(X))\ p\in 2^{X_{0}}\bigr\},

ordered by reverse inclusion. Suppose
\mathbb{G}
is
\mathbb{C}[X]
-generic over
V
and let
\mathfrak{G}=\bigcup_{p\in\mathbb{G}}p.
Then
\mathfrak{G}
is a function from
X

into
\{0,1\}
and
V[\mathbb{G}]=V[\mathfrak{G}]
. The function
\mathfrak{G}
is called a

Definition 33
.

Cohen generic function and we say that
\mathfrak{G}
is

Definition 34
.

Cohen over
V
.
Note that
\mathfrak{G}

has a canonical
\mathbb{C}[X]
-name
\dot{\mathfrak{G}}
, defined by

\dot{\mathfrak{G}}=\{(p,\dot{n})\,:\,n\in X,\,\ p\in\mathbb{C}[X],\text{ and }p(n)=1\}.

When
X=\omega
we call
\mathfrak{G}
a

Definition 35
.

Cohen generic real.

The following is standard.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
Jec03
, Lemma 14.35]
)
.

The partial order
\mathbb{C}[X]
has c.c.c.

We will be interested in understanding when a forcing notion is isomorphic to Cohen forcing over a cardinal.

Definition 3.2
.

A partial order
\mathbb{P}
is

Definition 36
.

Cohen when
\RO(\mathbb{P})\cong\mathbb{C}[X]
for some infinite set
X
.

The following results will allow us to determine when a partial order is Cohen.

Proposition 3.3

(
[
Jec03
, Comment after Theorem 30.10]
)
.

If
\mathbb{P}
is a non-trivial countable forcing notion, then
\mathbb{P}
is forcing isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}[\omega]
.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
Jec03
, Theorem 30.10]
)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{B}
is an infinite Boolean algebra of uniform density. Then
{\mathcal{B}}
is Cohen if and only if the set

\{\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{B}\,:\,|A|\leq\omega\text{ and }\mathbb{A}\text{ is a regular subalgebra of }\mathbb{B}\}

has a club subset
C
such that whenever
{\mathcal{A}}_{0},{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\in C
, if
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
is the Boolean algebra generated
(as a Boolean algebra)
by
A_{0}\cup A_{1}
,
then
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\in C
. Further, if the uniform density of
\mathbb{B}

is
\kappa
, then
{\mathcal{B}}\cong\mathbb{C}[\kappa]
.

3.2.
Lebesgue
\kappa
-measure

While we will eventually be interested in arbitrary
\Perm(X)
-invariant probability
\kappa
-measures on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
, there is one specific measure on
2^{X}
which is especially important and provides us with a good example for what is to follow.

Definition 3.5
.

Define the

Definition 37
.

Lebesgue
\kappa
-measure on
(2^{X},\sigma_{\kappa}(2^{X}))
to be the
probability
\kappa
-measure

\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}
such that for any
X_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(X)
and
\tau\in 2^{X_{0}}
, we have

\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}\bigl(\{f\in 2^{X}\,:\,\tau\subseteq f\}\bigr)=2^{-|X_{0}|}.

We will abuse notation and use
\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}
to denote the corresponding
probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{1}}(X)
, i.e., the pushforward of
\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}
along
\iota_{\Lang_{1},X}
.

Note that by
Proposition

2.18
, there is a unique probability
\kappa
-measure satisfying
Definition

3.5
.

Definition 3.6
.

For
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{1}:X}
we write
\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}(\gamma)
to mean
\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}(\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

.

We have the following immediate result.

Lemma 3.7
.

The measure
\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}
is
\Perm(X)
-invariant.

Suppose
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
and suppose
\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{1}:X}]^{V_{0}}
and
Y=\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}

. In general, we do not have that
\bigl[\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}(Y)\bigr]^{V_{0}}=\bigl[\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}(Y)\bigr]^{V_{1}}
. In fact, if
[2^{X}]^{V_{0}}
is countable in
V_{1}
then we have
\bigl[\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}((2^{X})^{V_{0}})\bigr]^{V_{1}}=0
.

The purpose of using
\kappa
-Borel codes instead of subsets when studying
Lebesgue
\kappa
-measure is that the Lebesgue
\kappa
-measure of a
\kappa
-Borel code is independent of the model of set theory in which we are working, as the following result
asserts. (We will not prove this here as it is a special case of
Theorem

5.12
applied to
\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{1}}(X)
, and we will not use this specific version beyond our discussion here.)

Proposition 3.8
.

Suppose
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
, let
X\in V_{0}
, and suppose

\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{1}:X}]^{V_{0}}
.
Then for any
r\in[0,1]^{V_{0}}
, we have

V_{0}\models\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}(\gamma)=r\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad V_{1}\models\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}(\gamma)=r.

In particular, if
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
and
X\in V_{0}
and
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{1}:X}]^{V_{0}}
then
V_{0}\models\gamma_{0}\preceq_{\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}}\gamma_{1}
if and only if
V_{1}\models\gamma_{0}\preceq_{\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X}}\gamma_{1}
. Hence we have
\bigl[{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X})\bigr]^{V_{0}}\subseteq\bigl[{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X})\bigr]^{V_{1}}

as partial order.

3.3.
Random Forcing

We now recall facts about random forcing. The relationship between random forcing and forcing with invariant measures is similar to the relationship between Cohen forcing and forcing with strong Fraïssé classes.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\kappa}(X)={\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\lambda^{(\kappa)}_{X})
. We call forcing with
\mathcal{R}_{\kappa}(X)

Definition 38
.

random
\kappa
-forcing over
X
.
Let
\dot{r}
be the
\mathcal{R}_{\kappa}(X)
-name

\displaystyle\dot{r}=\bigg\{\Bigl(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bigwedge_{i\in[k]}\psi(x_{i})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}},\,\dot{n}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\,:\,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\subseteq X\ \text{and}\ \bigwedge_{i\in[k]}\bigl(\psi(x_{i})\rightarrow U(n)\bigr)\

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\text{and}\ \neg\bigl(\bigwedge_{i\in[k]}\psi(x_{i})\rightarrow\bot\bigr)\bigg\}.

If
\mathbb{G}
is generic for
\mathcal{R}_{\kappa}(X)
then we call
\dot{r}^{\mathbb{G}}
the

Definition 39
.

random function associated to
\mathbb{G}
and we say
\dot{r}^{\mathbb{G}}
is

Definition 40
.

random over
V
. Further,
\dot{r}
is the canonical name of such a function. When
X=\omega
, we call
\dot{r}^{\mathbb{G}}
a

Definition 41
.

random real (over
V
).

The following lemmas are standard; see, e.g.,

[
BJ95
, Lemma 3.1.1]
and

[
Sch14
, Lemma 8.8]
.

Lemma 3.9
.

\mathcal{R}_{\kappa}(X)
is c.c.c.

Lemma 3.10
.

If
\mathbb{G}
is generic for
\mathcal{R}_{\kappa}(X)
then
V[\mathbb{G}]=V\bigl[\dot{r}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigr]
.

3.4.
\kappa
-Measurable Space Equivalence

In this paper we are interested in the connections between invariant measures on different spaces of structures as well as the structures which come from random forcing with invariant measures. However, if we are only interested in the space of structures as a
\kappa
-measurable space, then all classes of structures with languages and sets of the same size are cryptomorphic. The following immediate lemma, along with
Lemma

2.10
, expresses this fact.

Lemma 3.11
.

There is a bijection
\iota_{\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}}\colon\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{1}:\coprod_{R\in\Lang}\,X^{\arity(R)}}\to\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
such that for every
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{1}:\coprod_{R\in\Lang}\,X^{\arity(R)}}
, we have

•

\rank(\gamma)=\rank\bigl(\iota_{\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}}(\gamma)\bigr)
and

•

\iota_{\Lang,X}``[\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

,

where

is as in

2.10
.

4.
Forcing with Strong Fraïssé Classes

We now recall from
[
Gol19
]
and
[
Kos23
]
the notion of a Cohen generic structure as well as some properties of forcing with a strong Fraïssé class.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\bm{K}

be a strong Fraïssé class. Define
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
to be the partial order consisting of elements of
\bm{K}
whose underlying set
is a subset of
X
, with
\leq
defined by

{\mathcal{A}}\leq{\mathcal{B}}
if and only if
{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}
.
We say that a structure
\mathfrak{G}
is

Definition 42
.

Cohen generic for
\bm{K}
on
X
over
V
if there is a generic
\mathbb{G}
for
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
such that
\mathfrak{G}=\bigcup\mathbb{G}
.

The following is immediate.

Lemma 4.2
.

A strong Fraïssé class is non-trivial if and only if
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
is non-trivial as a forcing notion.

The following is
a key
result regarding Cohen generic structures.

Lemma 4.3

(
[
Gol19
, Lemma 2.3]
)
.

If
\bm{K}
is a strong Fraïssé class then
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
has c.c.c.

Given two strong Fraïssé classes
\bm{K}_{0}
and
\bm{K}_{1}
in
V
and a set
X\in V
,
a natural
question to consider
is: “When does the existence of a generic over
V
for
\bm{K}_{0}
ensure the existence of a generic over
V
for
\bm{K}_{1}
?” The following results give a partial answer.

Proposition 4.4
.

If
\bm{K}
is a (non-trivial) strong Fraïssé class over
\Lang
and
\mathbb{G}
is generic for
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
over
V
then there is an
\mathbb{H}
which is generic for
\mathbb{C}[X]
such that
\mathbb{H}\in V[\mathbb{G}]
.

Proof 4.5
.

As
\bm{K}
is non-trivial and has (HP) there exist

•

an
n\in\omega
,

•

an atomic formula
\psi\in\Lang
of arity
n
,

•

{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in\bm{K}
, and

•

enumerations (without repetitions)
\langle a_{i}\rangle_{i\in[n]}
of
A
and
\langle b_{i}\rangle_{i\in[n]}
of
B

for which

{\mathcal{A}}\models\psi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})
and
{\mathcal{B}}\models\neg\psi(b_{0},\dots,b_{n-1})
.

For
\alpha\in X
let
{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
be a structure where the map
a_{j}\mapsto(\alpha,j)
is an isomorphism from
{\mathcal{A}}
to
{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
.
Define
{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}
similarly.
Let
\iota\colon X\times[n]\to X
be a bijection.

Suppose
{\mathcal{M}}\in\bm{K}
with
M\subseteq X
.
Let

Z_{{\mathcal{M}}}=\{\ell\in X\,:\,\iota``[\{\ell\}\times[n]]\subseteq M\}
,
and let

p_{{\mathcal{M}}}\colon Z_{{\mathcal{M}}}\to\{0,1\}
be the map defined by

p_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\ell)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }{\mathcal{M}}\models\psi\bigl(\iota(\langle\ell,0\rangle),\iota(\langle\ell,1\rangle),\dots,\iota(\langle\ell,n-1\rangle)\bigr)\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
\mathbb{G}
is generic for
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
we let
\mathbb{H}=\{p_{{\mathcal{M}}}\,:\,{\mathcal{M}}\in\mathbb{G}\}
. It suffices to prove the following claim.

Claim 43
.

\mathbb{H}
is generic for
\mathbb{C}[X]
.

Proof 4.6
.

First note that
\mathbb{H}
is a filter as
\mathbb{G}
is a filter and
\bm{K}
has (HP). Now suppose
D\subseteq\mathbb{C}[X]
is dense. Let
D^{*}=\{{\mathcal{M}}\in\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})\,:\,p_{{\mathcal{M}}}\in D\}
.
Let
{\mathcal{N}}\in\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
. Let
E=\{{\mathcal{M}}\in\bm{K}\,:\,(\exists Y\subseteq X)\ \,M=\iota``[Y\times[n]]\}
. The set
E
is dense and so we can find an
{\mathcal{N}}^{*}\in\mathbb{G}\cap E
such that
{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq{\mathcal{N}}^{*}
.

Then there must be some
q\in D
such that
p_{{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}\subseteq q
. Let
\langle x_{i}\rangle_{i\in[s]}
be an enumeration of
\dom(q)\setminus\dom(p_{{\mathcal{N}}^{*}})
.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{-1}={\mathcal{N}}^{*}
. For
i\in[s]
let
{\mathcal{N}}_{i}^{0}
be a structure on
N_{i-1}\cup\{x_{i}\}\times[n]
containing
{\mathcal{N}}_{i-1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{x_{i}}
, and let
{\mathcal{N}}_{i}^{1}
be a structure on
N_{i-1}\cup\{x_{i}\}\times[n]
containing
{\mathcal{N}}_{i-1}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{x_{i}}
. Note that we can always find such structures as
\bm{K}
has (JEP) and (HP). Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{i}={\mathcal{N}}_{i}^{q(x_{i})}
.

We then have
p_{{\mathcal{N}}_{s-1}}=q
, and so
{\mathcal{N}}_{s-1}\in D^{*}
. Therefore
\mathbb{H}\cap D\neq\emptyset
. As
D
was an arbitrary dense subset of
\mathbb{C}[X]
, this proves the claim.

This completes the proof of

Proposition

4.4
.

The following is one of two partial converses to
Proposition

4.4
that we obtain.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let

\bm{K}_{0}
and
\bm{K}_{1}
be (non-trivial) strong Fraïssé classes over
\Lang
with only countably many isomorphism types, and
suppose

\mathbb{G}_{0}
is generic for
\mathfrak{C}_{\omega}(\bm{K}_{0})
.
Then there is a
\mathbb{G}_{1}\in V[\mathbb{G}_{0}]
which is generic for
\mathfrak{C}_{\omega}(\bm{K}_{1})
.

Proof 4.8
.

By
Proposition

3.3
, we have

\mathfrak{C}_{\omega}(\bm{K}_{0})\approx\mathbb{C}[\omega]\approx\mathfrak{C}_{\omega}(\bm{K}_{1})
,
from which the proposition follows.

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose
X
is a set and
\bm{K}
is a strong Fraïssé class with at most
|X|
-many isomorphism classes. Then
\RO(\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K}))
has uniform density
|X|
.

Proof 4.10
.

This is clear by our assumption on
\bm{K}
.

We then have our second partial converse to
Proposition

4.4
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Suppose
\bm{K}
is a strong Fraïssé class with at most
\omega_{1}
-many elements up to isomorphism. Then
\mathfrak{C}_{\omega_{1}}(\bm{K})
and
\mathbb{C}[\omega_{1}]
are forcing isomorphic, and in particular
\mathfrak{C}_{\omega_{1}}(\bm{K})
is Cohen.

Proof 4.12
.

Let
\mathbb{B}
be the
Boolean
subalgebra of
\RO(\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K}))
generated by
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
. Then

\mathbb{B}\approx\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
, so it suffices to show that
\mathbb{B}\approx\mathbb{C}[\omega_{1}].

Let
C=\{i_{\alpha}``[\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}(\bm{K})]\,:\,\alpha<\omega_{1}\}
, where
i_{\alpha}
is the canonical embedding of
\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}(\bm{K})
into
\mathbb{B}
.
Then
C
witnesses the club requested in

Theorem

3.4
for
\mathbb{B}
.

Remark 4.13
.

Note that
Propositions
4.7
and

4.11

fully answer
[
Kos23
, Question 1]
when the underlying set has size at most
\omega_{1}
.

5.
Lifting Invariant Measures

5.1.
Liftings and Relativizations of Invariant Measures

We begin by proving some basic facts about invariant measures on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.

Definition 5.1
.

Suppose
f\colon X\to Y
and
\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang
. Then we define
\pi_{f:\Lang_{0}}\colon\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Y)\to\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{0}}(X)
to be the map which takes an
\Lang
-structure
{\mathcal{M}}

with underlying set
Y

to the
\Lang_{0}
-structure
{\mathcal{N}}

with underlying set
X
such that

{\mathcal{N}}\models R(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad{\mathcal{M}}\models R(f(x_{0}),\dots,f(x_{n-1}))

for every
R\in\Lang_{0}
of arity
n
and
x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}\in X
.
For
X\subseteq Y
,
we will write
\pi_{X:\Lang_{0}}
to mean
\pi_{f:\Lang_{0}}
,
where
f\colon X\to Y
is the inclusion map.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\mu

be a probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Y)
.
For
f\colon X\to Y
and
\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang
, we write
\pi_{f:\Lang_{0}}^{*}(\mu)
to denote the “pushforward” of
\mu
along
\pi_{f:\Lang_{0}}
, i.e., the probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{0}}(X)
where

\pi_{f:\Lang_{0}}^{*}(\mu)(A)=\mu(\pi_{f:\Lang_{0}}^{-1}(A))

for every
A\in\sigma_{\kappa}(\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{0}}(X))
.

Note that a probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
is invariant if and only if
\pi_{g:\Lang}^{*}(\mu)=\mu

for all
g\in\Perm(X)
.
In fact, we have the following.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\mu
be a probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Suppose that

\alpha^{*}(\mu)=\mu

for every
\alpha\in\Perm^{<\omega}(X)
.
Then
\mu
is invariant.

Proof 5.4
.

Suppose
\beta\in\Perm(X)
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}
. There is some
\beta_{-}\in\Perm^{<\omega}(X)
such that
\beta(\gamma)=\beta_{-}(\gamma)
. Then
\mu(\gamma)=\mu(\beta_{-}(\gamma))=\mu(\beta(\gamma))
. Therefore, by
Proposition

2.18
, we have
\mu=\pi_{\beta:\Lang}^{*}(\mu)
.

The following immediate fact is
a key property
of invariant measures.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose

\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang
,
and let
n\in\omega
.
Suppose
i_{0},i_{1}\colon[n]\to X
are injective. Then for any invariant measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
, we have
\pi_{i_{0}:\Lang_{0}}^{*}(\mu)=\pi_{i_{1}:\Lang_{0}}^{*}(\mu)
.

Lemma

5.5
is
useful
because it will allow us to uniquely “lift” an invariant measure on one infinite set to an invariant measure on any other infinite set.

Definition 5.6
.

Suppose
\mu
is an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
. For
n\in\omega
, let
i_{n}\colon[n]\to X
be
an
injection. We define the

Definition 44
.

skeleton
of
\mu
to be

\boxcircle_{\mu}=\langle\pi_{i_{n}:\Lang_{0}}^{*}(\mu)\rangle_{n\in\omega,\,\Lang_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(\Lang)}.

We let
\boxcircle_{\mu:\Lang_{0},n}=\pi_{i_{n}:\Lang_{0}}^{*}(\mu)
.

Proposition 5.7
.

For any invariant measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Y)
, there is a unique invariant measure
\nu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
such that
\boxcircle_{\mu}=\boxcircle_{\nu}
.

Proof 5.8
.

Suppose
\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})
is a finite conjunction of literals in
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\omega}(\Lang_{0})
, where
\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang
, with parameters in
X
. Let
X_{0}=\{a_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, let
k=|X_{0}|
, and let
\zeta\colon X_{0}\to[k]

be a bijection. Let

\nu^{\circ}\bigl(\varphi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\bigr)=\boxcircle_{\mu:\Lang_{0},k}\bigl(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(\zeta(a_{0}),\dots,\zeta(a_{n-1}))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\bigr).

Note that as
\mu
is invariant, the value of
\nu^{\circ}
does not depend on the bijection
\zeta
. But then by
Proposition

2.18
there is a unique probability
\kappa
-measure
\nu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
such that

\nu^{\circ}(\gamma)=\nu(\gamma)

for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}
.

For any
\alpha
with finite support, we have
\alpha^{*}(\nu)=\nu
. Hence
\nu
is invariant by
Lemma

5.3
.

It is immediate from
the way

\nu
was constructed that
\boxcircle_{\mu}=\boxcircle_{\nu}
.

Definition 5.9
.

When
\mu
is an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Y)
we write
\mu_{X}
for the unique
invariant measure
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
such that
\boxcircle_{\mu}=\boxcircle_{\mu_{X}}
, and call
\mu_{X}
the

Definition 45
.

lifting of
\mu
to
X
.

As a consequence of
Proposition

5.7
, for any invariant measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
and any injection
i\colon\omega\to X
, the measure
\mu
is completely determined by
\pi_{i:\Lang}^{*}(\mu)
.

The following is also an immediate consequence of
Proposition

5.7
.

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be an injection and let
\mu
be an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(Y)
. Then
\pi_{f:\Lang}^{*}(\mu)=\mu_{X}
.

Because we will want to move between different models of set theory, it will be important to know, for
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
, when a probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu\in V_{0}
is the same as a probability
\kappa
-measure
\mu^{*}\in V_{1}
. The general notion of relativization is defined in
[
Ack10
]
, but for our purposes the following definition suffices.
(We will generally use square brackets when we need to parenthesize objects being relativized.)

Definition 5.11
.

Suppose
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
and suppose that for

i\in\{0,1\}
, we are given
a probability

\kappa
-measure

\mu_{i}\in V_{i}

on
[\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)]^{V_{i}}
.
We say that
\mu_{1}
is the

Definition 46
.

relativization of
\mu_{0}
to
V_{1}
if

\mu_{0}(\gamma)=\mu_{1}(\gamma)

for all
\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}
,
and in this case we write
[\mu_{0}]^{V_{1}}
for
\mu_{1}
.

Note that by
Proposition

2.18
and the fact that
[\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}=[\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{1}}
, any probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
in
V_{0}
has a unique relativization to
V_{1}
.

The following shows that a measure and its relativization always agree on the measure of any
\kappa
-Borel code in the domain of the original measure.

Theorem 5.12
.

Suppose

V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
.
Let
\kappa,X,\Lang\in V_{0}

and let

\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}
.
Suppose

\mu\in V_{0}
is a probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Then for any
r\in[0,1]^{V_{0}}
, we have

V_{0}\models\mu(\gamma)=r\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad V_{1}\models\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma)=r.

Proof 5.13
.

For
\gamma,\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
let
\gamma\triangle\zeta=(\gamma\wedge\neg\zeta)\vee(\neg\gamma\wedge\zeta)
.
Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a collection of models of set theory such that

•

V_{0}\subseteq V

for all
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

•

V_{0},V_{1}\in{\mathcal{V}}
, and

•

there is a
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
such that
V\,\models\,|X|=|\Lang|=|\kappa|=\omega
.

Note that for
V^{+},V^{-}\in{\mathcal{V}}
we have
[\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}]^{V^{+}}=[\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}]^{V^{-}}
. We will therefore refer to this set simply as
\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
.

We say that two
\kappa
-Borel codes
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}
are
\mu
-equivalent when

\mu^{V}(\gamma_{0})=\mu^{V}(\gamma_{1})

for all
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

Suppose that
\gamma=\bigvee\Gamma
is an almost basic
\kappa
-Borel code. Then there is an almost basic
\kappa
-Borel code
\gamma^{*}=\bigvee\Gamma^{*}

that is
\mu
-equivalent to
\gamma
such that
for any distinct
\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1}\in\Gamma^{*}
, we have
\mathord{\hbox to26.61pt{\vbox to12.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.30557pt\lower-4.44444pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
