\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{V}=\emptyset
for all
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
. Therefore

\mu(\gamma)=\mu^{V}(\gamma)

for all
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

Let
I\subseteq[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}
be the collection of
\gamma
such that

\mu(\gamma\wedge\zeta)=\mu^{V}(\gamma\wedge\zeta)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu(\gamma\vee\zeta)=\mu^{V}(\gamma\vee\zeta)

for any
\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
and any
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

Note that there are functions
A,O\colon\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}\times\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}\to\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
such that
A(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
is
\mu
-equivalent to
\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1}
and
O(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
is
\mu
-equivalent to
\gamma_{0}\vee\gamma_{1}
. Therefore
\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}\subseteq I
.

Also note that there is an
n\colon\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}\to\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
such that

n(\zeta)
is
\mu
-equivalent to
\neg\zeta

for all
\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
.
Suppose
\gamma\in I
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
. Then
\mu(\neg\gamma\vee\zeta)=1-\mu(\gamma\wedge\neg\zeta)
and
\mu(\neg\gamma\wedge\zeta)=1-\mu(\gamma\vee\neg\zeta)
. Therefore
I
is closed under negation. In particular for
\gamma\in I
, for
\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
and for any
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we have
\mu(\gamma\triangle\zeta)=\mu^{V}(\gamma\triangle\zeta)
.

Suppose
V^{*}\in{\mathcal{V}}
is such that
V^{*}\,\models\,|X|=|\Lang|=|\kappa|=\omega
. Then
V^{*}\models\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
is a standard Borel space. Hence for every
\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V^{*}}
and
\epsilon\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
there is an
\eta(\epsilon,\gamma)\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
such that
\mu^{V^{*}}(\gamma\triangle\zeta)<\epsilon
.

Now suppose
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in I
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
. For
\epsilon\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
we have
\mu^{V^{*}}(\gamma_{0}\triangle\eta(\epsilon,\gamma_{0}))<\epsilon
and
\mu^{V^{*}}(\gamma_{1}\triangle\eta(\epsilon,\gamma_{1}))<\epsilon
.
Therefore, as
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in I
, we have
\mu^{V}(\gamma_{0}\triangle\eta(\epsilon,\gamma_{0}))<\epsilon
and
\mu^{V}(\gamma_{1}\triangle\eta(\epsilon,\gamma_{1}))<\epsilon

for any
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
. Therefore for any
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we have

\bigl|\mu^{V}(\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1}\wedge\zeta)-\mu(\eta(\epsilon,\gamma_{0})\wedge\eta(\epsilon,\gamma_{1})\wedge\zeta)\bigr|<2\epsilon.

Then for any
V^{-},V^{+}\in{\mathcal{V}}
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
we have

\bigl|\mu^{V^{-}}(\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1}\wedge\zeta)-\mu^{V^{+}}(\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1}\wedge\zeta)\bigr|<4\epsilon.

But, as
\epsilon
was arbitrary, this implies
\mu^{V^{-}}(\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1}\wedge\zeta)=\mu^{V^{+}}(\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1}\wedge\zeta)
. A similar argument shows that
\mu^{V^{-}}((\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1})\vee\zeta)=\mu^{V^{+}}((\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1})\vee\zeta)
. Therefore
\gamma_{0}\wedge\gamma_{1}\in I
. As
I
is closed under negation and
\mu
-equivalence, this also shows that
\gamma_{0}\vee\gamma_{1}\in I
.

Now suppose
\gamma=\bigvee\Gamma
with
\gamma\in V_{0}
and
\Gamma\subseteq I
. For
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
we have
\mu^{V}(\gamma)=\sup\{\mu^{V}(\bigvee\Gamma_{0})\,:\,\Gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(\Gamma)\}
. But by the above
we know that
\mu^{V}(\bigvee\Gamma_{0})=\mu^{V_{0}}(\bigvee\Gamma_{0})
for any
\Gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(\Gamma)
. Hence
\mu(\bigvee\Gamma)=\mu^{V}(\bigvee\Gamma)
and
\bigvee\Gamma\in I
. Therefore,
I=[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}
.

The result then follows as

\mu(\gamma)=\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma)

for
\gamma\in I
.

By
Theorem

5.12
, for
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}

we have

[{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)]^{V_{0}}=[{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu^{V_{1}})]^{V_{1}}\cap V_{0}
, and for
\gamma,\zeta\in[{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)]^{V_{0}}
we have
V_{0}\models\gamma\preceq_{\mu}\zeta
if and only if
V_{1}\models\gamma\preceq_{\mu^{V_{1}}}\zeta
.
We also have the following
corollary.

Corollary 5.14
.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
and
\mu
is a probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
. Then for all
\epsilon\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
there is a
\zeta_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
such that
\mu(\gamma\triangle\zeta_{\epsilon})<\epsilon
.

Proof 5.15
.

Let
V_{1}
be such that
V_{1}\,\models\,|X|=|\Lang|=|\kappa|=\omega
. Then
[\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)]^{V_{1}}
is a standard Borel space and hence a Radon space. So for all
\epsilon\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
there must be a
\zeta_{\epsilon}\in[\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{1}}
such that
\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma\triangle\zeta_{\epsilon})<\epsilon
. The result then follows from
Theorem

5.12
and the fact that
\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
is the same in any model of set theory.

Note that for

V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
,
in general it need not be the case that if

\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}
and
V_{0}\models\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

then
V_{1}\models\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.58865pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.97 7.58 C -2.57 7.58 -2.37 8.76 -1.98 8.76 C -1.7 8.76 -1.45 8.18 -1.19 7.58 C -0.94 6.97 -0.69 6.4 -0.41 6.4 C -0.12 6.4 0.12 6.97 0.38 7.58 C 0.64 8.18 0.88 8.76 1.17 8.76 L 2.97 7.58}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

, as the following example shows.

Example 5.16
.

Let
\Lang=\{U,\preccurlyeq\}\cup\{f\}
where
U
is a unary relation symbol,
\preccurlyeq
is a binary relation symbol,
and
f
is a unary function symbol. For
\alpha,\beta\in\kappa^{+}
, let
T_{\alpha,\beta}
be the sentence of
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
which asserts the conjunction of the following statements.

•

\preccurlyeq
is a linear ordering
of order type
\alpha

on the elements that satisfy
U
.

•

\preccurlyeq
is a linear ordering
of order type
\beta

on the elements that satisfy
\neg U
.

•

U(x)\leftrightarrow\neg U(f(x))
for all
x
.

•

f(f(x))=x

for all
x
.

Note that
T_{\alpha,\beta}
implies that
f
is a bijection from the set of elements that satisfy
U
to the set of elements that satisfy
\neg U
. Hence
T_{\alpha,\beta}
has a model precisely when
|\alpha|=|\beta|
.

Hence for
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
, if

V_{0}\models\kappa>\omega
and
V_{1}\models|\kappa|^{V_{0}}=\omega
then

V_{0}\models\mathord{\hbox to45.27pt{\vbox to21.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.63582pt\lower-8.75006pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\kappa
-Borel codes is absolute.

Proof 5.18
.

This is because there is a first-order quantifier-free formula
\psi
of arity
k
and elements
x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1}
such that

V\models\mathord{\hbox to5.18pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{}{}}

Definition 5.19
.

\gamma_{0}\sqsubseteq\gamma_{1}
if
\mathord{\hbox to36.55pt{\vbox to11.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.27452pt\lower-3.75pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\emptyset
. We say that
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}
are

Definition 47
.

equivalent if
\gamma_{0}\sqsubseteq\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{1}\sqsubseteq\gamma_{0}
.

Note that by
Lemma

5.17
,
equivalence of almost basic Borel codes is absolute.

Theorem 5.20
.

Suppose
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
and

X,Y\in V_{0}
. Let

\mu\in V_{0}
be a probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Then the following hold.

(a)

There is a unique probability
\kappa
-measure

\mu^{V_{1}}\in V_{1}
that is the relativization of
\mu
.

(b)

\mu
is invariant if and only if
\mu^{V_{1}}
is invariant.

(c)

If
\eta\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}
then
\mu(\eta)=\mu^{V_{1}}(\eta)
.

(d)

If
\mu
is invariant then
[\mu_{Y}]^{V_{1}}=(\mu^{V_{1}})_{Y}
.

(e)

If
\mu
is invariant and
i\colon X\to Y
is injective then

\mu(\gamma)=\mu_{Y}(i(\gamma))

for any
\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}
.

Proof 5.21
.

We have (a) by
Proposition

2.18
, and (b) by
Lemma

5.3
. We have (c) by
Theorem

5.12
, and (d) by Propositions
2.18
and
5.7
. Finally, (e) follows from a straightforward induction on the complexity of Borel codes.

Note that for
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
, if
X
and
Y
are each of size at most
\kappa
, then
the
\kappa
-Borel code in
\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
which defines
{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket}_{X}^{\Lang}
is in general different from the
\kappa
-Borel code in
\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:Y}
that defines
{\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket}_{Y}^{\Lang}
, because quantifiers are converted to conjunctions and disjunctions.
However, the next result shows that the almost-sure theory of an invariant measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
is the same as the almost-sure theory of
\mu_{Y}
. Further, this theory is independent of the model of set theory in which one is working.

Theorem 5.22
.

Suppose
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
.
Let
\varphi\in[\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)]^{V_{0}}
be a sentence.
Suppose

X,Y\in V_{0}
with
V_{0}\models\max\{|X|,|Y|\}\leq\kappa
.
Then for any invariant measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
, we have

\mu(\varphi)=1\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mu_{Y}(\varphi)=1,

i.e.,

[\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)]^{V_{0}}=[\ThOp_{\kappa}([\mu_{Y}]^{V_{1}})]^{V_{1}}\cap V_{0}.

Proof 5.23
.

First note that for any
\varphi\in[\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)]^{V_{0}}
, the equivalence between
\varphi\in[\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)]^{V_{0}}
and
\varphi\in[\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu^{V_{1}})]^{V_{1}}
follows from
Lemma

2.15
and
Theorem

5.12
.

But if
|X|=|Y|
then
\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)=\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu_{Y})
.
Therefore if
V_{1}\subseteq V^{*}
and
V^{*}\models|X|=|Y|
, we have

\displaystyle[\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)]^{V_{0}}

\displaystyle=[\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu^{V^{*}})]^{V^{*}}\cap V_{0}

\displaystyle=[\ThOp_{\kappa}((\mu^{V^{*}})_{Y})]^{V^{*}}\cap V_{0}

\displaystyle=[\ThOp_{\kappa}([\mu_{Y}]^{V^{*}})]^{V^{*}}\cap V_{0}

\displaystyle=[\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu_{Y})]^{V_{0}}.

The result then follows.

If
\Lang
is countable and
{\mathcal{M}}
is a countably infinite
\Lang
-structure then there is a sentence
\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}}\in\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1},\omega}(\Lang)
, called a Scott sentence of
{\mathcal{M}}
, such that a countably infinite

\Lang
-structure
satisfies
\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}}
if and only if it is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{M}}
. Therefore for any countably infinite
X_{0}\subseteq X
, if
\mu_{X_{0}}
is concentrated on the isomorphism class of
{\mathcal{M}}
, then
for any countably infinite
X_{1}\subseteq X
the measure
\mu_{X_{1}}
is concentrated on the isomorphism class of
{\mathcal{M}}
,
by
Theorem

5.22
.

Remark 5.24
.

Suppose
\Lang
contains a relation symbol

R
of arity
n+1
. There is a sentence
\psi_{R}^{\mathrm{f}}
which holds in any
\Lang
-structure
{\mathcal{M}}

if and only if
R^{\mathcal{M}}
is the graph of a function with
n
inputs. There is also a sentence
\psi_{R}^{\mathrm{cf}}
which holds in any
\Lang
-structure
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if
R^{\mathcal{M}}
is the graph of a choice function with
n
inputs.

By
[
AFP17
]
,
for any relation symbol
R\in\Lang
and any invariant measure
\mu
, we have
(\psi_{R}^{\mathrm{f}}\rightarrow\psi_{R}^{\mathrm{cf}})\in\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)
. In other words,

\mu
assigns measure 1 to the set of structures in which any function defined by a relation must be a choice function.
Therefore, relaxing the assumption that our language is relational
would not add much
generality
to
the study of invariant measures.

5.2.
Dissociated, Ergodic and Extreme Invariant Measures

In this subsection we show that for any invariant measure, being dissociated, ergodic, or extreme are all equivalent notions.
We do this by showing that each of these notions is absolute and then using
Proposition

2.27
.

Proposition 5.25
.

Suppose
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
, and let
\mu\in V_{0}
be an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Then
\mu
is dissociated in
V_{0}
if and only if
\mu^{V_{1}}
is dissociated in
V_{1}
.

Proof 5.26
.

First note that if
\mu^{V_{1}}
is dissociated
then
\mu
is dissociated
by
Theorem

5.12
.

Now suppose
\mu
is dissociated.
Let
X_{0},X_{1}\subseteq X
be disjoint finite sets and let
A_{0}\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X_{0}}
and

A_{1}\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X_{1}}
be in
V_{1}
. By
Corollary

5.14
, for each
\epsilon\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
there
exist

\gamma^{0}_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X_{0}}
and
\gamma^{1}_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X_{1}}

such that
\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{i}\triangle\gamma_{\epsilon}^{i})<\epsilon
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. Hence

|\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{0}\cap A_{1})-\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma_{\epsilon}^{0}\wedge\gamma_{\epsilon}^{1})|\leq 2\epsilon

and

|\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{0})\cdot\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{1})-\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma_{\epsilon}^{0})\cdot\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma_{\epsilon}^{1})|<3\epsilon.

But
\mu
is dissociated
and so

|\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma_{\epsilon}^{0}\wedge\gamma_{\epsilon}^{1})-\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma_{\epsilon}^{0})\cdot\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma_{\epsilon}^{1})|=0.

Therefore we have

|\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{0}\cap A_{1})-\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{0})\cdot\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{1})|<5\epsilon.

Because
\epsilon
was arbitrary, this implies that
\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{0}\cap A_{1})=\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{0})\cdot\mu^{V_{1}}(A_{1})
. Hence
\mu^{V_{1}}
is dissociated.

We now
show that
extremality
of
an invariant measure
is absolute.
We will first need a combinatorial lemma.

Lemma 5.27
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\leqslant)

be a directed partial order (i.e., a poset in which every finite collection of elements has an upper bound),
and let
F
be a presheaf on
(\mathscr{C},\leqslant)
.
For
A\leqslant B
, write

i_{A,B}
for the corresponding map in
(\mathscr{C},\leqslant)
when treated as a category.
For
A\leqslant B
and
b\in F(B)
, write
b\!\restriction_{A}
for
F(i_{A,B})(b)
.

Suppose that

0<|F(A)|<\omega

for all
A\in\mathscr{C}
.
Then there is a sequence
\langle a_{A}\rangle_{A\in\mathscr{C}}

such that

•

a_{A}\in F(A)

for all
A\in\mathscr{C}
and

•

a_{A}=a_{B}\!\restriction_{A}

whenever
A\leqslant B\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof 5.28
.

Let
\Lang
be the language containing

•

a sort
S_{A}
for each
A\in\mathscr{C}
,

•

a constant symbol
c_{a}
of sort
S_{A}
for each
a\in F(A)
,

•

a function symbol
f_{A,B}
from
S_{B}
to
S_{A}

for each
A\leqslant B
, and

•

a constant symbol
d_{A}

for each
A\in\mathscr{C}
.

Let
T
be the theory which says,
for each
A\leqslant B\in\mathscr{C}
, that

•

(\forall x:S_{A})\bigvee_{a\in F(A)}(x=c_{a})
,

•

f_{A,B}(c_{b})=c_{b\,\!\restriction_{A}}
, and

•

d_{A}=f_{A,B}(d_{B})
.

Suppose
T_{0}
is a finite subset of
T
.
Note that
T_{0}
mentions
d_{A}
for at most finitely many elements
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n-1}\in\mathscr{C}
.
As
\mathscr{C}
is directed, there must be some
B
such that
A_{i}\leqslant B
for all
i\in[n]
.
Further, as
F(B)\neq\emptyset
, there is some
b\in F(B)
.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
be an
\Lang
-structure with underlying set
\{(A,a)\,:\,a\in F(A)\}
where
c_{a}^{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}=(A,a)
for
a\in F(A)
.
Let

d_{A_{i}}^{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}=c_{b\,\!\restriction_{A_{i}}}^{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}

for
i\in[n]
.
For
B\in\mathscr{C}\setminus\{A_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, let
d_{B}^{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}
be an arbitrary element of
M_{0}
of the form
(B,b)
, which must exist as
F(B)\neq\emptyset
.
Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
clearly satisfies
T_{0}
. As
T_{0}
was arbitrary,
T
is first-order and finitely satisfiable, and hence has a model
{\mathcal{M}}
.
But then for all
A\in\mathscr{C}
there is an
a_{A}
such that
{\mathcal{M}}\models d_{A}=c_{a_{A}}
.
Hence
\langle a_{A}\rangle_{A\in\mathscr{C}}

is the desired collection.

Proposition 5.29
.

Suppose
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
, and let
\mu\in V_{0}
be an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
. Then
\mu
is extreme in
V_{0}
if and only if
\mu^{V_{1}}
is extreme in
V_{1}
.

Proof 5.30
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be the collection of triples
(n,\Lang_{0},X_{0})

where
n\in\omega
, where
\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang
is finite, and where
X_{0}\subseteq X
is finite. Let
(n_{0},\Lang_{0},X_{0})\leqslant(n_{1},\Lang_{1},X_{1})
if and only if
n_{0}\leq n_{1}
and
\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang_{1}
and
X_{0}\subseteq X_{1}
. Note that
(\mathscr{C},\leqslant)
is directed.

Define an
\epsilon
-approximate measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{0}}(X_{0})
to be a map

\nu\colon\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang_{0}:X_{0}}\to[0,1]
such that

•

\nu(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\bigwedge\emptyset\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})\geq 1-\epsilon
,

•

if
\varphi
and
\psi
are finite conjunctions of
\Lang_{0}
-literals with
\models\varphi({\bm{x}})\rightarrow\psi({\bm{x}})
, then

\nu^{\circ}(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\psi({\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})+\epsilon\geq\nu^{\circ}(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi({\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})-\epsilon

for every
{\bm{a}}\in X_{0}
,

•

\nu(\psi({\bm{a}})\wedge\neg\psi({\bm{a}}))\leq\epsilon

for any
\Lang_{0}
-literal
\psi({\bm{x}})
and
{\bm{a}}\in X_{0}
, and

•

for all tuples
{\bm{a}}\in X_{0}
and
sequences
\langle\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\rangle_{i\in[n+1]}

of
\Lang_{0}
-literals, we have

\bigg|\nu^{\circ}\bigg(\bigwedge_{i\in[n]}\psi_{i}({\bm{a}})\bigg)-\mu^{\circ}\bigg(\psi_{n}({\bm{a}})\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in[n]}\psi_{i}({\bm{a}})\bigg)+\mu^{\circ}\bigg(\neg\psi_{n}({\bm{a}})\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in[n]}\psi_{i}({\bm{a}})\bigg)\bigg|\leq\epsilon.

For a measure
\nu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang_{0}}(X_{0})
and
n\in\omega
we let

a(\nu,n)(\gamma)=\frac{\lfloor 2^{n+1}\cdot\nu(\gamma)\rfloor}{2^{n+1}}.

Note that
a(\nu,n)
is a
2^{-n}
-approximate measure.

For
q\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
let
F_{q}
be the presheaf on
\mathscr{C}
where

F_{q}(n,\Lang_{0},X_{0})
is the collection of tuples
(\nu,\eta,r,s)
such that

•

\nu
and
\eta
are
2^{-n}
-approximate measures,

•

r,s\in\{\frac{k}{2^{n}}\}_{0<k<2^{n}}

where

r+s=1
and

q<r,s
,

•

for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang_{0}:X_{0}}
,

\circ

\nu(\gamma),\eta(\gamma)\in\{\frac{k}{2^{n}}\}_{0\leq k\leq 2^{n}}
and

\circ

|\mu(\gamma)-(r\cdot\nu(\gamma)+s\cdot\eta(\gamma))|<2^{-n+2}
.

If
(n_{0},\Lang_{0},X_{0})\leqslant(n_{1},\Lang_{1},X_{1})
and
(\nu,\eta,r,s)\in F(n_{1},\Lang_{1},X_{1})
let
(\nu,\eta,r,s)\!\restriction_{(n_{0},\Lang_{0},X_{0})}
be the unique element
(\nu_{0},\eta_{0},\frac{\lfloor 2^{n_{0}}\cdot r\rfloor}{2^{n_{0}}},\frac{\lfloor 2^{n_{0}}\cdot s\rfloor}{2^{n_{0}}})
for which
\nu_{0}(\gamma)=\frac{\lfloor 2^{n_{0}}\cdot\nu(\gamma)\rfloor}{2^{n_{0}}}
and
\eta_{0}(\gamma)=\frac{\lfloor 2^{n_{0}}\cdot\eta(\gamma)\rfloor}{2^{n_{0}}}
for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang_{0}:X_{0}}
.

Suppose that
\mu
is not extreme, i.e., there are distinct invariant
measures
\nu,\eta
and

r,s\in(0,1)
such that
\mu=r\cdot\nu+s\cdot\eta
. Let
q\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
be such that
q<r,s
. Then for any
(n_{0},X_{0},\Lang_{0})\in\mathscr{C}
, we have

\Bigl(a(\nu,{n_{0}}),a(\eta,n_{0}),\frac{\lfloor 2^{n_{0}}\cdot r\rfloor}{2^{n_{0}}},\frac{\lfloor 2^{n_{0}}\cdot s\rfloor}{2^{n_{0}}}\Bigr)\in F(n_{0},X_{0},\Lang_{0}),

and hence
F_{q}(n_{0},X_{0},\Lang_{0})
is not empty.

Now suppose that
q\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
and
F_{q}(n_{0},X_{0},\Lang_{0})\neq\emptyset
for every
(n_{0},X_{0},\Lang_{0})\in\mathscr{C}
. By
Lemma

5.27
there is a function
g
with domain
\mathscr{C}
where
g(A)\in F_{q}(A)
for
A\in\mathscr{C}
and where
g(B)\!\restriction_{A}=g(A)
for
A\leqslant B\in\mathscr{C}
. If
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}
then there must be an
\Lang_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(\Lang)
and
X_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(X)
such that
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang_{0}:X_{0}}
. Define

•

\nu^{\circ}(\gamma)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{0}(g(n,\Lang_{0},X_{0}))(\gamma)
,

•

\eta^{\circ}(\gamma)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{1}(g(n,\Lang_{0},X_{0}))(\gamma)
,

•

r=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{2}(g(n,\Lang_{0},X_{0}))
, and

•

s=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{3}(g(n,\Lang_{0},X_{0}))
,

where
\pi_{i}
is the projection to coordinate
i
of its
4
-tuple input.
Such limits always exist by the definition of
F_{q}
. Further, because of how

\nu^{\circ}
and
\eta^{\circ}
were defined, by
Proposition

2.18
there are unique
probability
\kappa
-measures
\nu,\eta
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
such that
\nu
agrees with
\nu^{\circ}
on
\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}
and
\eta
agrees with
\eta^{\circ}
on
\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}
. But also, for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}
, we have

\mu(\gamma)=r\cdot\nu^{\circ}(\gamma)+s\cdot\eta^{\circ}(\gamma)=r\cdot\nu(\gamma)+s\cdot\eta(\gamma).

Hence

\mu=r\cdot\nu+s\cdot\eta

by
Proposition

2.18
.

Therefore
\mu
is not extreme if and only if there is some
q\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
such that for all
A\in\mathscr{C}
we have
F_{q}(A)\neq\emptyset
. But in fact, for each
q\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
, we have
[F_{q}]^{V_{0}}=[F_{q}]^{V_{1}}
. Hence
\mu
is extreme in
V_{0}
if and only if it is extreme in
V_{1}
.

We now characterize

\mu
-almost invariant sets
in terms of Borel codes.

Lemma 5.31
.

Let
\mu
be an invariant
measure
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
and suppose
\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
satisfies

\mu(h[\gamma]\triangle\gamma)=0

for all
h\in\Perm^{<\omega}(X)
.
Then
\gamma
is
\mu
-almost invariant.

Proof 5.32
.

Let
g\in\Perm(X)
. For
\epsilon>0
, by
Corollary

5.14
there is some
\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
such that
\mu(\gamma\triangle\zeta)<\epsilon
. As
\zeta\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:X}
, there is an
h\in\Perm^{<\omega}(X)
such that
h[\zeta]=g[\zeta]
. Therefore

\displaystyle\mu(\gamma\triangle g[\gamma])

\displaystyle\leq\mu(\gamma\triangle h[\gamma])+\mu(h[\gamma]\triangle h[\zeta])+\mu(h[\zeta]\triangle g[\zeta])+\mu(g[\zeta]\triangle g[\gamma])

\displaystyle=0+\epsilon+0+\epsilon

\displaystyle=2\epsilon.

As
\epsilon
was arbitrary,
\gamma
is
\mu
-almost invariant.

Proposition 5.33
.

Suppose
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
and suppose
\mu\in V_{0}
is an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
. Then
\mu
is ergodic in
V_{0}
if and only if
\mu^{V_{1}}
is ergodic in
V_{1}
.

Proof 5.34
.

By

Theorem

5.12

and
Lemma

5.31
,
a given
\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}
is
\mu
-almost invariant (in
V_{0}
) if and only if it is
\mu^{V_{1}}
-almost invariant (in
V_{1}
). Therefore, because
[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{0}}\subseteq[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{1}}
, if
\mu^{V_{1}}
is ergodic (in
V_{1}
) then
\mu
is ergodic (in
V_{0}
).

Claim 48
.

If
\mu^{V_{1}}
is non-ergodic (in
V_{1}
) then
\mu_{\omega}
is non-ergodic (in
V_{0}
).

Proof 5.35
.

Suppose
\mu^{V_{1}}
is non-ergodic, i.e., there is a
\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V_{1}}
such that

\mu^{V_{1}}(\gamma)\not\in\{0,1\}

and

\mu^{V_{1}}(g[\gamma]\triangle\gamma)=0

for all
g\in[\Perm^{<\omega}(X)]^{V_{1}}
.

Let
V^{*}
be a model of set theory where
V_{1}\subseteq V^{*}
and where
V^{*}\models|X|=|\Lang|=\omega
. Note that
[\Perm^{<\omega}(X)]^{V_{1}}=[\Perm^{<\omega}(X)]^{V^{*}}
, and so by
Lemma

5.31
, the code
\gamma
witnesses that
\mu^{V^{*}}
is not ergodic. But as
V^{*}\models|X|=\omega
, this implies that
(\mu^{V^{*}})_{\omega}

is not ergodic. Hence

[\mu_{\omega}]^{V^{*}}
is not ergodic by
Theorem

5.20
(d).
Let
q\in{{\mathbb{Q}}}^{+}
be such that
q<[\mu_{\omega}]^{V_{*}}(\gamma)<1-q
.

We now define a tree
(T,\preceq)
where level
n
of
T
consists of pairs
(n,\zeta)

such that
there is a

\gamma\in\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{ab}}_{\Lang:\omega}
for which

•

q-2^{-n}<\mu_{\omega}(\gamma)<1-q+2^{-n}
, and

•

\mu_{\omega}(\zeta\triangle g[\zeta])<2^{-n}

for all
g\in\Perm^{<\omega}(\omega)
.

We let

(m,\zeta_{m})\prec_{1}(n,\zeta_{n})
if and only if
n=m+1
and
\mu(\zeta_{m}\triangle\zeta_{n})\preceq 2^{-m}
. We let
\preceq
be the transitive closure of
\prec_{1}
(along with equality).

Note that
(T,\preceq)
is the same whether it is defined in
V_{0}
or
V^{*}
. Also note that

(T,\preceq)
is ill-founded
in
V^{*}
,
and so
(T,\preceq)
is ill-founded in
V_{0}
.

Suppose
\langle(k,\zeta_{k})\rangle_{k\in\omega}
is an infinite branch of
(T,\preceq)
in
V_{0}
. Let
\eta=\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}\bigvee_{k\geq n}\zeta_{k}
.
Note that for any
n\in\omega
and
g\in\Perm^{<\omega}(X)
we have

\mu_{\omega}\bigg(\bigvee_{k\geq n}\zeta_{k}\triangle g\bigg[\bigvee_{k\geq n}\zeta_{k}\bigg]\bigg)\leq\sum_{k\geq n}\mu_{\omega}\bigg(\zeta_{k}\triangle g[\zeta_{k}]\bigg)\leq\sum_{k\geq n}2^{-k}=2^{-n+1}.

Therefore
\mu_{\omega}(\eta\triangle g[\eta])=0
.

Let
n\in\omega
be such that
2^{-n+1}<q
. We then have

\mu_{\omega}(\zeta\triangle\gamma_{n})\leq\sum_{k\geq n}\mu_{\omega}(\gamma_{n}\triangle\gamma_{k})\leq\sum_{k\geq n}2^{-k}=2^{-n+1}.

Hence
0<\mu_{\omega}(\zeta)<1
and
\zeta
witnesses that
\mu_{\omega}
is not ergodic, completing the proof of the claim.

Now for an infinite set
X_{0}
and an injection
i\colon X_{0}\to X
, if
\mu
is ergodic then
\mu_{X_{0}}
is ergodic as well by
Theorem

5.20
(e)
and the fact that
\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
.

In particular, if
\mu_{\omega}
is not ergodic then
\mu
is not ergodic either. Hence if
\mu^{V_{1}}
is not
ergodic then
\mu
is not ergodic.
Therefore ergodicity is absolute, completing the proof of the proposition.

One of the reasons we are interested in ergodic invariant measures is
that they give rise to complete theories, as we show in the next lemma.

Lemma 5.36
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
be a sentence
and suppose
|X|\leq\kappa
.
Let
\mu
be an ergodic invariant
measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
. Then either
\varphi\in\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)
or
\neg\varphi\in\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)
.

Proof 5.37
.

This follows immediately from the fact that for any
g\in\Perm(X)
, the codes
g[\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi({\bm{x}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}]
and
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi({\bm{x}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
interpret the same set.

In other words, every ergodic invariant measure has a complete almost-sure theory in
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{+},\omega}(\Lang)
.

Putting the above results together,
we have the following equivalences with ergodicity.

Theorem 5.38
.

Suppose
\mu
is an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
with
\Lang,X,\mu\in V
. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

V\models\mu\text{ is ergodic}
.

(b)

V^{*}\models\mu^{V^{*}}\text{ is ergodic}
,
for some model of set theory
V^{*}
with
V\subseteq V^{*}
.

(c)

V^{*}\models\mu^{V^{*}}\text{ is ergodic}
,
for any model of set theory
V^{*}
with
V\subseteq V^{*}
.

(d)

V\models\mu_{Y}\text{ is ergodic}
,
for any infinite set
Y\in V
.

(e)

V\models\mu\text{ is extreme}
.

(f)

V\models\mu\text{ is dissociated}
.

Proof 5.39
.

The implication from (c) to (b) is immediate. The implications from (b) to (a) and from (a) to (c) both follow from
Proposition

5.33
.

Let
V^{*}
be a model of set theory where
V\subseteq V^{*}
and where
V^{*}\models|\Lang|=|X|=|Y|=\omega
.
We then have

V^{*}\models\mu\text{ is ergodic if and only if }\mu_{Y}\text{ is ergodic}.

Therefore, by the equivalence of (a) and (b) we have that (a) and (d) are equivalent as well.

By
Proposition

2.27
,
we also have that

V^{*}\models\mu\text{ is ergodic if and only if }\mu\text{ is extreme}

and

V^{*}\models\mu\text{ is ergodic if and only if }\mu\text{ is dissociated}.

Therefore by
Propositions
5.25
and
5.29
, we have
that (a) is equivalent to (e) and that (a) is equivalent to (f).

6.
Random Generic Structures

In this section we consider forcing with an invariant measure.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mu
be a probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
. Define a

Definition 49
.

\mu
-random generic filter over
V
to be a filter on
{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)
that is generic over
V
.

and

, then we need not have

In fact, the following theorem shows that the intersection of the interpretations of elements of a
\mu
-random generic filter, which is necessarily closed under
\sim_{\mu}
, always contains a unique element.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\mu\in V
be a probability
\kappa
-measure on
[\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)]^{V}
, and suppose
\mathbb{G}
is a
\mu

V
. Let
\mathbb{G}_{b}=\mathbb{G}\cap\mathbb{B}^{{\kappa},\mathfrak{b}}_{\Lang:X}
.
Then the following hold.

Proof 6.3
.

Note that (b) follows from (a) and (c). We first prove (a). Suppose
\Lang_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(\Lang)
and
X_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{<\omega}(X)
, and let
{\bm{a}}
be an enumeration of
X_{0}
. For
{\mathcal{M}}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{0}}(X_{0})
let
\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{x}})
be a first-order quantifier-free formula of arity
|X_{0}|
such that

•

{\mathcal{M}}\models\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{a}})
, and

•

if
{\bm{b}}
is an enumeration of
X_{0}
and
{\mathcal{N}}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{0}}(X_{0})
where
{\mathcal{N}}\models\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{b}})
then the map
{\bm{a}}\mapsto{\bm{b}}
is an isomorphism from
{\mathcal{M}}
to
{\mathcal{N}}
.

Note that we can always find such a
\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}}
as both
X_{0}
and
\Lang_{0}
are finite. Let

P({\mathcal{M}})=\{\gamma\in[{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)]^{V}\,:\,\gamma\preceq_{\mu}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{a}})\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\}.

We then let

P(X_{0},\Lang_{0})=\bigcup_{{\mathcal{M}}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{0}}(X_{0})}P({\mathcal{M}}).

Note that
P(X_{0},\Lang_{0})
is dense in
{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)
. Therefore there must be some
Z(X_{0},\Lang_{0})\in\mathbb{G}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{X_{0},\Lang_{0}}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{0}}(X_{0})
with
Z(X_{0},\Lang_{0})\in P({\mathcal{M}}_{X_{0},\Lang_{0}})
. Further, if
{\mathcal{M}}^{*}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang_{0}}(X_{0})
and
Z^{\prime}(X_{0},\Lang_{0})\in\mathbb{G}\cap P({\mathcal{M}}^{*})
then we must have
{\mathcal{M}}^{*}={\mathcal{M}}_{X_{0},\Lang_{0}}
as
\mathbb{G}
is a filter and all elements are compatible. Therefore we have
|\bigcap_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b}}\mathord{\hbox to5.13pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.56422pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.93 7.58 C -2.54 7.58 -2.34 8.76 -1.95 8.76 C -1.66 8.76 -1.42 8.18 -1.16 7.58 C -0.9 6.97 -0.66 6.4 -0.37 6.4 C -0.09 6.4 0.16 6.97 0.42 7.58 C 0.67 8.18 0.92 8.76 1.2 8.76 L 2.93 7.58}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

.

If
X_{0}\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq X
and
\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang_{1}\subseteq\Lang
then
{\mathcal{M}}_{X_{1},\Lang_{1}}\!\restriction_{X_{0},\Lang_{0}}={\mathcal{M}}_{X_{0},\Lang_{0}}
. Hence there is an
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}\in[\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(X)]^{V[\mathbb{G}]}
such that for any finite
X_{0}\subseteq X
and finite
\Lang_{0}\subseteq\Lang
, we have
{\mathcal{M}}_{G}\!\restriction_{X_{0},\Lang_{0}}={\mathcal{M}}_{X_{0},\Lang_{0}}
. Therefore
\{{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}\}=\bigcap_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b}}\mathord{\hbox to5.13pt{\vbox to9.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.56422pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.93 7.58 C -2.54 7.58 -2.34 8.76 -1.95 8.76 C -1.66 8.76 -1.42 8.18 -1.16 7.58 C -0.9 6.97 -0.66 6.4 -0.37 6.4 C -0.09 6.4 0.16 6.97 0.42 7.58 C 0.67 8.18 0.92 8.76 1.2 8.76 L 2.93 7.58}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and so (a) holds.

We now prove (c). It
suffices to show that for any
\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V}

we have

\displaystyle\gamma\in\mathbb{G}

Towards the converse

\gamma=\bigvee\Gamma

\gamma\in\mathbb{G}

D_{\gamma}=\{\neg\gamma\}\cup\{\eta\in{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)\,:\,(\exists\zeta\in\Gamma)\,(\eta\preceq_{\mu}\zeta)\}.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}

\nu\not\preceq_{\mu}\neg\gamma
, i.e.,
with

\mu(\gamma\wedge\nu)>0
. Then, as
\mu(\gamma\wedge\nu)\leq\sum_{\zeta\in\Gamma}\mu(\zeta\wedge\nu)

\zeta\in\Gamma
with
\mu(\zeta\wedge\nu)>0
. Hence
(\zeta\wedge\nu)\in D_{\gamma}
(and in particular
(\zeta\wedge\nu)\in{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)
) with
\zeta\wedge\nu\preceq_{\mu}\nu
. Therefore
D_{\gamma}
is dense, and so there must be some
\beta\in\mathbb{G}\cap D_{\gamma}
. However, as
\gamma\in\mathbb{G}

\zeta\in\Gamma

\beta\preceq_{\mu}\zeta
. Therefore, there must be some
\zeta\in\Gamma\cap\mathbb{G}

Proposition 6.5
.

Suppose that
X,\Lang\in V
and that
V\models|X|\leq\kappa
.
Let
\mu\in V
be a probability
\kappa

[\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)]^{V}
and let
\mathbb{G}
be a
\mu
-random generic filter over
V
. Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}\models[\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)]^{V}
.

An ergodic invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(X)
is said to be

Definition 51
.

properly

Definition 52
.

ergodic when it assigns measure
0
to the isomorphism class of each structure in
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(X)
.
In
[
AFKP17a
]

it was shown that for every properly ergodic invariant measure
\mu\in V
, there is no model in
V
of
[\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)]^{V}
.
However,
Proposition

6.5
establishes that
for any
\mu
-random generic filter
\mathbb{G}
, there is such a model in
V[\mathbb{G}]
,
even though
V
and
V[\mathbb{G}]
have the same cardinals.

In the case of
non-properly
ergodic invariant measures,
we have the following consequence of

Proposition

6.5
.
Recall that two
\Lang
-structures are
potentially isomorphic
when they satisfy the same sentences of
\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\omega}(\Lang)
.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\Lang
be a countable language, and
let
\mu
be an invariant
measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(\omega)

that concentrates on the isomorphism class of some structure.
Suppose
\mathbb{G}
is a
\mu_{X}
-random generic filter over
V
and
suppose
\mathbb{H}
is a
\mu_{Y}
-random generic filter over
V
.
Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{H}}
are potentially isomorphic.

Proof 6.8
.

Suppose
\mu
is concentrated on the isomorphism class of
{\mathcal{N}}
. Because
\Lang
is countable there is a sentence
\varphi_{{\mathcal{N}}}\in\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1},\omega}(\Lang)
such that for any
{\mathcal{N}}^{*}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(\omega)
, we have
{\mathcal{N}}^{*}\models\varphi
if and only if
{\mathcal{N}}^{*}\cong{\mathcal{N}}
. In particular,
\mu
concentrates on

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and hence
\varphi_{{\mathcal{N}}}\in\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu)
.

Therefore, by
Theorem

5.22
, we have that
\varphi_{{\mathcal{N}}}\in\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu_{X})
and
\varphi_{{\mathcal{N}}}\in\ThOp_{\kappa}(\mu_{Y})
. So, by
Proposition

6.5
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}\models\varphi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{H}}\models\varphi_{{\mathcal{N}}}
. Hence
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{H}}
are potentially isomorphic.

For a countable language
\Lang
,
suppose
\mu
is a properly ergodic invariant probability measure
on
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(\omega)
. If
\mathbb{G}
is a
\mu_{X}
-random generic and
\mathbb{H}
is a
\mu_{Y}
-random generic,
then
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}
need not be potentially isomorphic to
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{H}}
, as the following example shows.

Example 6.9
.

Suppose
\Lang=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
where
U_{i}
is a unary relation symbol for each
i\in\omega
. Let
\mu
be the measure where
{\llbracket U_{i}(n)\rrbracket}_{\omega}=\frac{1}{2}
for all
i,n\in\omega
and where all such events are independent.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be generic for the measure
\mu\times\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(\omega)\times\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(\omega)
. Let
\mathbb{G}_{0}=\{X\,:\,X\times\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(\omega)\in\mathbb{G}\}
and
\mathbb{G}_{1}=\{X\,:\,\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(\omega)\times X\in\mathbb{G}\}
. Note that both
\mathbb{G}_{0}
and
\mathbb{G}_{1}
are generic for
\mu
.

Consider the event

E_{n_{0},n_{1}}=\Bigl\{{\mathcal{N}}_{0}\times{\mathcal{N}}_{1}\in\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(\omega)\times\StrMathOp_{\Lang}(\omega)\,:\,\bigwedge_{i\in\omega}\bigl[{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\models U_{i}(n_{0})\leftrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\models U_{i}(n_{1})\bigr]\Bigr\}.

Note that
(\mu\times\mu)(E_{n_{0},n_{1}})=0
for all
n_{0},n_{1}\in\omega
. Therefore we must have
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}_{0}}\not\cong{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}_{1}}
.

The ideas in
Example

6.9
can be combined with the results of
[
AFKP17a
]
to show that for any properly ergodic
invariant probability
measure
\mu
on
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(\omega)
, whenever
\mathbb{G}
is generic for
\mu\times\mu
and
\mathbb{G}_{0},\mathbb{G}_{1}
are as in

Example

6.9
,
we have

{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}_{0}}\not\cong{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}_{1}}
.

The following is a key property
of
\mu
-random forcings.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\mu
be a probability
\kappa
-measure
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Then
{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)
has c.c.c.

Proof 6.11
.

Suppose
\langle\gamma_{\alpha}:\alpha<\omega_{1}\rangle\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)
is a sequence of pairwise incompatible elements. In particular, for each
\alpha\neq\beta
we have
\mu(\gamma_{\alpha}\wedge\gamma_{\beta})=0
.
For some
n<\omega
, the set

\{\alpha<\omega_{1}:\mu(\gamma_{\alpha})>\frac{1}{n}\}
is uncountable. Let
\alpha_{0}<\cdots<\alpha_{n}<\omega_{1}
be such that
\mu(\gamma_{\alpha_{i}})>\frac{1}{n}
, for all
i\leq n
. Then

\mu\bigg(\bigvee_{i\in[n+1]}\gamma_{\alpha_{i}}\bigg)=\sum_{i\in[n+1]}\mu(\gamma_{\alpha_{i}})>\frac{n+1}{n}>1,

a contradiction.

The following tells us that from the perspective of asking which generics exist, forcing with any non-atomic invariant probability measure
is as good as
forcing with any other,
provided the languages and sets are countably infinite.

Proposition 6.12
.

For
i\in\{0,1\}
, suppose that

\mu_{i}
is an invariant
probability
measure on
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang_{i}}(X_{i})
which assigns measure
0
to each trivial structure.
Let
V
be a model of set theory such that

X_{i},\Lang_{i},\mu_{i}\in V
and

V\models|X_{i}|=\omega\qquad\text{and}\qquad V\models|\Lang_{i}|\leq\omega

for each
i\in\{0,1\}
.
Let
\mathbb{G}_{0}
be a
\mu_{0}
-random generic.
Then there is a
\mathbb{G}_{1}\in V[\mathbb{G}_{0}]
which is
\mu_{1}
-random generic over
V
.

Proof 6.13
.

Because

V\models\max\{|X_{i}|,|\Lang_{i}|\}=\omega

for
i\in\{0,1\}
,
by Lemmas
2.10
and

3.11
there must be an isomorphism between
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang_{i}}(X_{i})
and
2^{\omega}
which lifts to an isomorphism of Borel codes. Further, as
\mu_{i}
is invariant and assigns measure
0
to each trivial structure,
\mu_{i}
has no point masses, i.e., is non-atomic. Suppose
\nu_{i}
is the measure on
2^{\omega}
which corresponds
to
\mu_{i}
. Then
\nu_{i}
is non-atomic as well. Hence by Maharam’s theorem there is a measure-preserving bijection
f
from
2^{\omega}
to itself such that
\nu_{0}=f^{*}(\nu_{1})
. The result follows immediately.

7.
Distinguishing Generic Structures

In this section we consider, for
\mu
an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
, how much information about
\mu
we can recover from a single
\mu
-random generic filter. In particular, we will be interested in
determining
when we can uniquely identify
\mu
from any
\mu
-random generic filter.

Unfortunately,
from a single
\mu
-random generic filter,
we cannot completely identify
\mu

among the invariant measures on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
This is because for any invariant measure
\mu^{*}
,
every
\mu
-random generic filter is also a

\frac{1}{2}(\mu+\mu^{*})
-random generic filter,
as the following result shows.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\mu
be a probability
\kappa
-measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Suppose
X,\Lang,\mu\in V
.
Let
\mathbb{G}
be a
\mu
-random generic filter and let
\gamma\in\mathbb{G}
.
For
\zeta\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V}
define
\nu(\mathord{\hbox to4.38pt{\vbox to12.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.1875pt\lower-4.44444pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.1875pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.41 9.41 C -2.02 9.41 -1.82 10.59 -1.43 10.59 C -1.14 10.59 -0.9 10.01 -0.64 9.41 C -0.38 8.8 -0.14 8.22 0.15 8.22 L 2.41 9.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

. Then the following hold.

(a)

\nu
is a probability
\kappa
-measure
on
(\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X))^{V}
.

(b)

If
\mu
is invariant and
\gamma
is
\mu
-almost invariant, then
\nu
is invariant.

(c)

\mathbb{G}
is a
\nu
-random generic filter over
V
.

Proof 7.2
.

To prove (a), note that as
\gamma\in\mathbb{G}
we have
\mu(\gamma)>0
.

To prove (b), suppose
\gamma
is
\mu
-almost invariant and
g\in[\Perm(X)]^{V}
.
Then

\nu(g[\zeta])=\frac{\mu(g[\zeta]\wedge\gamma)}{\mu(\gamma)}=\frac{\mu(g[\zeta]\wedge g[\gamma])}{\mu(g[\gamma])}=\frac{\mu(\zeta\wedge\gamma)}{\mu(\gamma)}=\nu(\zeta).

Finally, to prove (c), suppose
D
is dense in
{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\nu)
. Let
D^{*}=D\cup\{\zeta\preceq_{\mu}\neg\gamma\}
. Suppose
\zeta\in{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)
, and hence
\mu(\zeta)>0
. Then either
\mu(\zeta\wedge\gamma)>0
or
\mu(\zeta\wedge\neg\gamma)>0
. But if
\mu(\zeta\wedge\gamma)>0
then
\zeta\in{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\nu)
.
Therefore
D^{*}
is dense in
{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)
.

Let
\zeta\in{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\nu)
. Then
\nu(\zeta)>0
and so
\mu(\zeta\wedge\gamma)>0
. Therefore
\zeta\wedge\gamma\in{\mathcal{O}}^{+}(\mu)
and so there must be an
\eta\in D^{*}\cap\mathbb{G}
with
\eta\preceq_{\mu}\zeta\wedge\gamma
. But then
\eta\in D
. Hence
\mathbb{G}
is a
\nu
-random generic filter.

Lemma

7.1
(b) and (c) suggest that if we want to be able to identify an invariant measure from a single random generic filter for the measure, we should restrict attention to measures
\mu
that assign measure
0
or
1
to
every
\mu
-almost invariant set,
i.e., which are ergodic.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
\varphi
be a first-order non-redundant quantifier-free
\Lang
-formula. For
{\mathcal{N}}
a finite
\Lang
-structure,
define the

Definition 53
.

density of
\varphi
in
{\mathcal{N}}
by

t({\mathcal{N}},\varphi)=\frac{|\{{\bm{x}}\in N^{(\arity(\varphi))}\,:\,{\mathcal{N}}\models\varphi({\bm{x}})\}|}{|N^{(\arity(\varphi))}|}.

For
I\subseteq[0,1]
and
i\colon\omega\to X
let

L(i,\varphi,I)=\bigl\{{\mathcal{M}}\in\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)\,:\,\lim_{n\to\infty}t({\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi)\in I\bigr\}.

In particular,
L(i,\varphi,[0,1])=\{{\mathcal{M}}\in\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)\,:\,\lim_{n\to\infty}t({\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi)\text{ exists}\}
.

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -22.07 10.17 C -21.67 10.17 -21.48 11.36 -21.08 11.36 C -20.8 11.36 -20.55 10.78 -20.29 10.17 C -20.04 9.57 -19.79 8.99 -19.51 8.99 C -19.22 8.99 -18.98 9.57 -18.72 10.17 C -18.46 10.78 -18.22 11.36 -17.93 11.36 C -17.65 11.36 -17.4 10.78 -17.14 10.17 C -16.89 9.57 -16.64 8.99 -16.36 8.99 C -16.07 8.99 -15.83 9.57 -15.57 10.17 C -15.31 10.78 -15.07 11.36 -14.78 11.36 C -14.5 11.36 -14.25 10.78 -13.99 10.17 C -13.74 9.57 -13.49 8.99 -13.21 8.99 C -12.92 8.99 -12.68 9.57 -12.42 10.17 C -12.16 10.78 -11.92 11.36 -11.63 11.36 C -11.35 11.36 -11.1 10.78 -10.85 10.17 C -10.59 9.57 -10.34 8.99 -10.06 8.99 C -9.77 8.99 -9.53 9.57 -9.27 10.17 C -9.01 10.78 -8.77 11.36 -8.48 11.36 C -8.2 11.36 -7.95 10.78 -7.7 10.17 C -7.44 9.57 -7.19 8.99 -6.91 8.99 C -6.62 8.99 -6.38 9.57 -6.12 10.17 C -5.86 10.78 -5.62 11.36 -5.33 11.36 C -5.05 11.36 -4.8 10.78 -4.55 10.17 C -4.29 9.57 -4.04 8.99 -3.76 8.99 C -3.47 8.99 -3.23 9.57 -2.97 10.17 C -2.71 10.78 -2.47 11.36 -2.18 11.36 C -1.9 11.36 -1.65 10.78 -1.4 10.17 C -1.14 9.57 -0.89 8.99 -0.61 8.99 C -0.32 8.99 -0.08 9.57 0.18 10.17 C 0.43 10.78 0.68 11.36 0.97 11.36 C 1.25 11.36 1.5 10.78 1.75 10.17 C 2.01 9.57 2.26 8.99 2.54 8.99 C 2.83 8.99 3.07 9.57 3.33 10.17 C 3.58 10.78 3.83 11.36 4.11 11.36 C 4.4 11.36 4.65 10.78 4.9 10.17 C 5.16 9.57 5.4 8.99 5.69 8.99 C 5.97 8.99 6.22 9.57 6.48 10.17 C 6.73 10.78 6.98 11.36 7.26 11.36 C 7.55 11.36 7.8 10.78 8.05 10.17 C 8.31 9.57 8.55 8.99 8.84 8.99 C 9.12 8.99 9.37 9.57 9.63 10.17 C 9.88 10.78 10.13 11.36 10.41 11.36 C 10.7 11.36 10.94 10.78 11.2 10.17 C 11.46 9.57 11.7 8.99 11.99 8.99 C 12.38 8.99 12.58 10.17 12.97 10.17 L 22.07 10.17}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
is the collection of
{\mathcal{M}}

n
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)

such that for some interval of length
2^{-k}
, the values of
t({\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi)
are always eventually in that interval.
Because
I
is open, we can then let
\zeta(i,\varphi,I)=\alpha(i,\varphi,I)\wedge\bigwedge_{k\in\omega}\beta_{k}(i,\varphi)
.

The following is a useful property about sampling from invariant measures.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
\mu
be an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Let
i\colon\omega\to X
be an injection,
and let
\varphi({\bm{x}})
be a first-order quantifier-free
\Lang
-formula.
Suppose
{\mathcal{M}}
is a
\mu
-random
\Lang
-structure.
Then for every
\epsilon>0
, there is some value
k(\epsilon,\varphi)

such that

\mu\bigl(\bigl\{{\mathcal{M}}\,:\,(\forall m>k(\epsilon,\varphi))\,\bigl|t({\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{i[m]},\varphi)-\mu(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(|{\bm{x}}|-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})\bigr|<\epsilon\bigr\}\bigr)>1-\epsilon.

Proof 7.7
.

This follows from a union bound applied to a straightforward generalization to finite relational structures of the concentration result

[
Lov12
, Theorem 10.3]
.

Thus we have the following result.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
\mu
be an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Let
i\colon\omega\to X
be an injection,
and let
\varphi({\bm{x}})
be a first-order quantifier-free
\Lang
-formula.
If
\mu
is ergodic,
then the set

L\bigl(i,\varphi,\bigl\{\mu(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(\arity(\varphi)-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\bigr\}\bigr)

has
\mu
-measure
1
.

Proof 7.9
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and let

I_{\epsilon}=\Bigl(\mu\bigl(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(\arity(\varphi)-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\bigr)-\epsilon,\ \mu\bigl(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(\arity(\varphi)-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\bigr)+\epsilon\Bigr).

For
n\in\omega
, let
\ell_{n}=k(\epsilon\cdot 2^{-n-1},\varphi)
where
k
is as in
Proposition

7.6
. Let

J_{n,\epsilon}=\bigl\{{\mathcal{M}}\,:\,(\exists m>\ell_{n})\,\bigl|t({\mathcal{M}}\!\restriction_{i[m]},\varphi)-\mu(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(\arity(\varphi)-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\bigr|\geq\epsilon\cdot 2^{-n-1}\bigr\}.

Then
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)\setminus\bigcup_{n\in\omega}J_{n,\epsilon}\subseteq L(i,\varphi,I_{\epsilon})
.

But by
Proposition

7.6
we have that
\mu(J_{n})\leq\epsilon\cdot 2^{-n-1}
. Therefore
\mu(L(i,\varphi,I_{\epsilon}))\geq 1-\epsilon
. As
\epsilon
was arbitrary and

\bigcap_{\epsilon>0}I_{\epsilon}=\bigl\{\mu(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(\arity(\varphi)-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\bigr\},

we have
\mu\Bigl(L\bigl(i,\varphi,\{\mu(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(\arity(\varphi)-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\})\bigr)\Bigr)=1
.

In particular, this implies that if
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
are distinct ergodic invariant measures
and
\mathbb{G}
is either a generic for
\mu_{0}
or a generic for
\mu_{1}
, we can determine
the measure for which
\mathbb{G}
is a generic
by looking at
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}
.

Corollary 7.10
.

Let
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
be distinct ergodic invariant measures
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
. Then there is some
U\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
of rank 5 such that
\mu_{0}(U)=1
and
\mu_{1}(U)=0
.

Proof 7.11
.

Let

i\colon\omega\to X
be an injection.
As
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
are distinct invariant measures
there must be a first-order quantifier-free
\Lang
-formula
\psi

such that

\mu_{0}(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\psi(i(0),\dots,i(n-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}})\neq\mu_{1}(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\psi(i(0),\dots,i(n-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}),

where
n
is the arity of
\psi
.
The result then follows from
Lemma

7.4
and
Proposition

7.8
.

This immediately implies the following corollary.

Corollary 7.12
.

Let
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
be distinct ergodic invariant measures
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
If
\mathbb{G}
is a
\mu_{0}
-random generic filter, then
\mathbb{G}
is not a
\mu_{1}
-random generic filter.

In general, when
\mu
is not ergodic,
L(\varphi,\{\mu({\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket}_{\omega})\})
need not have
\mu
-measure
1
.
In other words, there might not be a single limiting probability to which almost all samples converge.
However, any invariant measure will concentrate on the collection of structures for which there is almost always some limiting value.

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
\mu
be an invariant measure
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Then
\mu(\zeta(i,\varphi,[0,1]))=1
for any injection
i\colon\omega\to X
and first-order quantifier-free
\Lang
-formula
\varphi
.

Proof 7.14
.

By
Theorem

5.12
and
Lemma

7.4
, and by moving to a larger model of set theory if needed, we can assume that
|X|=|\Lang|=|\kappa|=\omega
.

By
[
Kal05
, Theorem A1.3]
, the measure
\mu
is a mixture of ergodic invariant
measures. But by
Proposition

7.8
, every ergodic invariant
measure assigns measure
1
to
\zeta(i,\varphi,[0,1])
. Therefore
\mu
is a mixture of measures for which
\zeta(i,\varphi,[0,1])
has measure
1
, and hence
\mu(\zeta(i,\varphi,[0,1]))=1
.

Definition 7.15
.

A structure
{\mathcal{M}}
is

Definition 54
.

highly homogeneous if whenever
A,B\subseteq{\mathcal{M}}
with
|A|=|B|<\omega
, there is some
g\in{\mathrm{{Aut}}}({\mathcal{M}})
such that
g``[A]=B
.
A strong Fraïssé class is

Definition 55
.

highly homogeneous when any two structures in
the class
of the same size
are isomorphic.

Note that a strong Fraïssé class is highly homogeneous precisely when it has a highly homogeneous Fraïssé limit.

Up to interdefinability, there are only five countably infinite highly homogeneous structures, namely the reducts of
({{\mathbb{Q}}},\leq)
, as shown in
[
Cam76
]
. Highly homogeneous structures are important for us because of the following result.

Proposition 7.16

(
[
AFKP17b
]
)
.

The following are equivalent for a countably infinite structure
{\mathcal{M}}
in a countable language
\Lang
.

•

{\mathcal{M}}
is highly homogeneous.

•

There is a unique invariant
probability
measure concentrated on the isomorphism class of
{\mathcal{M}}
in
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(\omega)
, and further this invariant probability measure is ergodic.

Given a generic filter that is either a Cohen generic filter for a strong Fraïssé class that is not highly homogeneous or
that
is a
\mu
-random generic filter,

Theorem

7.21

will allow us to
determine which of these is the case. This will be a consequence of the following result.

Theorem 7.17
.

Let
\bm{K}
be a strong Fraïssé class of
\Lang
-structures
that is not highly homogeneous.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{G}
be the result of forcing with
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K})
, i.e., Cohen forcing with
\bm{K}

on
X
. Suppose
i\colon\omega\to X
is an injection. Then there is a first-order quantifier-free

\Lang
-formula

\varphi
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,t({\mathcal{M}}_{G}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi)\neq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\,t({\mathcal{M}}_{G}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi).

In particular, we have

{\mathcal{M}}_{G}\not\in\bigcap\{\zeta(i,\psi,[0,1])\,:\,\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\omega}(\Lang)\text{ is quantifier-free}\}.

Proof 7.18
.

First we prove two claims.

Claim 56
.

There is
some
first-order
quantifier-free
\Lang
-formula
\varphi

of arity
k>0
and there are
rationals
q_{0}
and
q_{1}

with

0<q_{0}<q_{1}<1

such that for all
n\in\omega
, there exist
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{n},{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n}\in\bm{K}
with

•

|{\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{n}|=|{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n}|>n
, and

•

t({\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{n},\varphi)<q_{0}<q_{1}<t({\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n},\varphi)
.

Proof 7.19
.

First note that whether or not this claim holds is independent of the model of set theory. Therefore, by moving to a forcing extension if needed, we can assume without loss of generality that
|\Lang|=|X|=\omega
and that
\bm{K}
has only countably many isomorphism types.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a countably infinite Fraïssé limit of
\bm{K}
. Because
\bm{K}
has two non-isomorphic elements of the same finite size,
{\mathcal{N}}
is not highly homogeneous. Therefore by
Proposition

7.16
there are two distinct ergodic invariant
probability
measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
on
\StrMathOp^{\omega}_{\Lang}(i``(\omega))
concentrated on the isomorphism class of
{\mathcal{N}}
.
Hence there must be some first-order quantifier-free

\Lang
-formula

\varphi
of arity
k\in\omega
and rationals
q_{0},q_{1}
such that

\mu_{0}\bigl(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(k-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\bigr)\ <\ q_{0}\ <\ q_{1}\ <\ \mu_{1}\bigl(\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\varphi(i(0),\dots,i(k-1))\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\bigr).

Note that because
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
are both concentrated on the isomorphism class of
{\mathcal{N}}
, the formula
\varphi
cannot be a sentence. The claim then follows from
Proposition

7.6
.

Now fix
q_{0},q_{1}
,
k
,
\varphi
, and
A_{0}^{n}
,
A_{1}^{n}
for
n\in\omega
, as in
56
.

Claim 57
.

For every
{\mathcal{B}}\in\bm{K}
there exist
{\mathcal{C}}_{0},{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\in\bm{K}
satisfying

•

{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
,

•

|C_{0}|=|C_{1}|
, and

•

t({\mathcal{C}}_{0},\varphi)\leq q_{0}<q_{1}\leq t({\mathcal{C}}_{1},\varphi)
.

Proof 7.20
.

Let
\epsilon>0
.
Let
n_{\epsilon}
be such that

\frac{|[n_{\epsilon}]^{(k)}|}{|[n_{\epsilon}+|B|]^{(k)}|}>1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and

\frac{|B|\cdot|[n_{\epsilon}+|B|]^{(k-1)}|}{|[n_{\epsilon}+|B|]^{(k)}|}<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

Recall that
\bm{K}
is a strong Fraïssé class, and hence has the joint embedding property. For
j\in\{0,1\}
let
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
be any structure of size
|B|+|A_{j}^{n_{\epsilon}}|
such that both
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}^{n_{\epsilon}}
have non-overlapping
embeddings into
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
.
We then have

\displaystyle\frac{t({\mathcal{A}}_{j}^{n_{\epsilon}},\varphi)\cdot|(A_{j}^{n_{\epsilon}})^{(k)}|}{|C_{j}^{(k)}|}\,

\displaystyle\leq\,t({\mathcal{C}}_{j},\varphi)

\displaystyle\leq\,\frac{t({\mathcal{A}}_{j}^{n_{\epsilon}},\varphi)\cdot|(A_{j}^{n_{\epsilon}})^{(k)}|+|B|\cdot|[|B|+|A_{j}^{n_{\epsilon}}|]^{(k-1)}|}{|C_{j}^{(k)}|}

where the left inequality follows by assuming no tuple in
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
with an element from
B
satisfies
\varphi
and the right inequality follows by assuming all tuples in
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
which have an element from
B
satisfy
\varphi
.

We therefore have

\displaystyle q_{1}-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,

\displaystyle<\,t({\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n_{\epsilon}},\varphi)-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\,t({\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n_{\epsilon}},\varphi)\cdot(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})

\displaystyle<\,\frac{t({\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{n_{\epsilon}},\varphi)\cdot|(A_{1}^{n_{\epsilon}})^{(k)}|}{|C_{1}^{(k)}|}

\displaystyle\leq\,t({\mathcal{C}}_{1},\varphi).

Because
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary, this implies that
q_{1}\leq t({\mathcal{C}}_{1},\varphi)
.
We also have

\displaystyle t({\mathcal{C}}_{0},\varphi)\,

\displaystyle\leq\,\frac{t({\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{n_{\epsilon}},\varphi)\cdot|(A_{0}^{n_{\epsilon}})^{(k)}|}{|C_{0}^{(k)}|}+\frac{|[|B|+|A_{0}^{n_{\epsilon}}|]^{(k-1)}|}{|C_{0}^{(k)}|}

\displaystyle<\,t({\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{n_{\epsilon}},\varphi)+\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}

\displaystyle<\,q_{0}+\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}.

Hence, as
\epsilon
was arbitrary, we have
t({\mathcal{C}}_{0},\varphi)\leq q_{0}
, proving the claim.

Now let
D_{0}=\{{\mathcal{E}}\in\bm{K}\,:\,t({\mathcal{E}},\varphi)\leq q_{0}\}
. By
57
,
D_{0}
is dense, and so we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}({\mathcal{M}}_{G}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi)\,\leq\,q_{0}
.

Let
D_{1}=\{{\mathcal{E}}\in\bm{K}\,:\,t({\mathcal{E}},\varphi)\geq q_{1}\}
. By
57
, we also have that
D_{1}
is dense. Hence
\limsup_{n\to\infty}({\mathcal{M}}_{G}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi)\geq q_{1}
.

Therefore

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\,({\mathcal{M}}_{G}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi)\,\leq\,q_{0}\,<\,q_{1}\,\leq\,\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,({\mathcal{M}}_{G}\!\restriction_{i``[n]},\varphi),

as desired, establishing the theorem.

Theorem 7.21
.

There is a
U\in\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}
with
\rank(U)=5
such that if
{\mathcal{M}}
is Cohen generic on
X
for some strong Fraïssé class of
\Lang
-structures
that is not highly homogeneous then
{\mathcal{M}}\not\in\mathord{\hbox to7.92pt{\vbox to10.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.95901pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

, and if

is

on

then

.

Proof 7.22
.

Let

U=\bigwedge\{\zeta(i,\varphi,[0,1])\,:\,\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\omega}(\Lang)\text{ is quantifier-free}\}
.
The result then follows from
Proposition

7.13
and
Theorem

7.17
.

The following is an immediate corollary of
Theorem

7.21
.

Corollary 7.23
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{M}}
is Cohen generic on
X
for some strong Fraïssé class of
\Lang
-structures
that is not highly homogeneous. Then
{\mathcal{M}}
is not
\nu
-random generic for any invariant
measure

\nu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.

Similarly if
{\mathcal{M}}
is
\nu
-random generic for some invariant
measure

\nu
on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
then
{\mathcal{M}}
is not Cohen generic on
X
for any strong Fraïssé class of
\Lang
-structures that is not highly homogeneous.

8.
Conjectures

We end with some conjectures about
\mu
-random generics and Cohen generics.

As we saw in
Proposition

4.7
, whenever
\bm{K}_{0}
and
\bm{K}_{1}
are non-trivial countable strong Fraïssé classes in countable languages, if
\mathbb{G}_{0}
is generic for
\bm{K}_{0}
on
X
over
V
then
V[\mathbb{G}_{0}]
contains a filter
\mathbb{G}_{1}
which is generic for
\bm{K}_{1}
on
\omega
over
V
. We conjecture that countability is not needed.

Conjecture 8.1
.

Suppose

\bm{K}_{0}
and
\bm{K}_{1}
are non-trivial Fraïssé classes each with at most
|X|
-many elements up to isomorphism. Then
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K}_{0})
and
\mathfrak{C}_{X}(\bm{K}_{1})
are forcing equivalent.

We also saw in
Proposition

6.12
that when the set and language are countably infinite, any two invariant probability measures that assign measure
0
to every trivial structure give rise to equivalent notions of forcing. This suggests the following conjecture.

Conjecture 8.2
.

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in V
be invariant measures on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
, and let
\mathbb{G}_{0}
be a
\mu_{0}
-random generic filter over
V
.
Suppose that
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
each assign measure
0
to every trivial structure.
Then in
V[\mathbb{G}_{0}]
there is a
\mu_{1}
-random generic filter over
V
.

We also make a stronger conjecture about the nature of invariant measures, motivated by the proof of
Proposition

6.12
.

Conjecture 8.3
.

Let
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
be invariant measures on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
Suppose that
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
each assign measure
0
to every trivial structure.
Then there is a measurable isomorphism
f\colon\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)\to\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
such that
\mu_{0}
is the pushforward of
\mu_{1}
along
f
, i.e.,
\mu_{0}=f^{*}(\mu_{1})
.

An important theorem relating random forcing and Cohen forcing is that adding a Cohen real will not add any random reals, and adding a random real will not add any Cohen reals (see
[
Jec87
, Lemma 3.3]
). We conjecture that this extends to arbitrary strong Fraïssé classes.

Conjecture 8.4
.

Let
\bm{K}
be a strong Fraïssé class in
\Lang
, and let

\mu
be an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
which concentrates on the collection of models potentially isomorphic to a Fraïssé limit of
\bm{K}
.

(1)

Suppose
\mathbb{G}_{0}
is a
\mu
-random generic filter over
V
. Then

V[\mathbb{G}_{0}]
contains no Cohen generic structures for
\bm{K}
on
X
over
V
.

(2)

Suppose
\mathbb{G}_{1}
is a
\bm{K}
-generic filter for
X
over
V
. Then

V[\mathbb{G}_{1}]
contains no
\mu
-random generic structures over
V
.

An interesting property of Cohen generic structures is that even when the Fraïssé limit of the corresponding age has a large automorphism group, the Cohen generic structures are rigid (see
[
Gol19
]
and
[
Kos23
]
for examples and
[
AGM23
]
for a property which ensures rigidity). This suggests that a similar phenomenon might occur for
\mu
-random generic structures.

Definition 8.5
.

Let
n\geq 1
.
A Fraïssé class

\bm{K}
has the

Definition 58
.

disjoint
n
-amalgamation property (abbreviated
n
-DAP) if whenever
\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\rangle_{i\in[n]}\subseteq\bm{K}
are such that

{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\!\restriction_{A_{i}\cap A_{j}}={\mathcal{A}}_{j}\!\restriction_{A_{i}\cap A_{j}}

for all
i<j\in[n]
,
there is some
{\mathcal{B}}\in\bm{K}
such that

{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}

for all
i\in[n]
.

Observe that
2
-DAP is the same as the strong amalgamation property.

Conjecture 8.6
.

Let
\bm{K}
be a strong Fraïssé class that satisfies
n
-DAP for every
n
and has only countably many isomorphism types. Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a countably infinite Fraïssé limit of
\bm{K}
. Suppose that
\mu
is an invariant measure concentrated on the isomorphism class of
{\mathcal{N}}
and that
\mathbb{G}
is a
\mu
-random generic filter. Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{G}}
is rigid.

We showed in

Theorem

7.21

that whenever a strong Fraïssé class
\bm{K}
is not highly homogeneous, there is some Borel set that contains all Cohen generic structures but contains no
\mu
-random generic structure for any invariant measure
\mu
. We conjecture that this holds for all non-trivial strong Fraïssé classes.

Conjecture 8.7
.

For any non-trivial strong Fraïssé class
\bm{K}
,
there is a
\gamma\in[\mathbb{B}^{\kappa}_{\Lang:X}]^{V}
such that

•

whenever

\mathbb{G}
is
\mu
-random generic for some invariant measure

\mu
that concentrates on the collection of models that are potentially isomorphic to a Fraïssé limit of
\bm{K}
.

Note that one cannot remove the assumption that
\bm{K}
is non-trivial, as for any trivial strong Fraïssé class there is a unique structure with age
\bm{K}

for each underlying set.

Recall from
Proposition

2.29
that
when
X
,
\Lang
, and
\kappa
are countable, every invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
is a mixture of ergodic ones. We conjecture that the same is true even when
X
,
\Lang
, or
\kappa
is uncountable.

Conjecture 8.8
.

Suppose
\mu
is an invariant measure on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
. Then
\mu
is a mixture
of ergodic invariant measures on
\StrMathOp^{\kappa}_{\Lang}(X)
.
